# PrusaSLA roadmap

This is the working todo list for turning this PrusaSlicer fork into an SLA-focused slicer. It's meant to be worked through over time, one todo per session, with OpenCode (or any coding agent) or by hand.

- **Background and rules:** [PLAN.md](PLAN.md). Codebase map (section 1), color palette (2.1), file ownership and hotspot files (3.3), definition of done (3.5).
- **Agent instructions:** [/AGENTS.md](../../AGENTS.md). OpenCode loads this file automatically.
- **Tracking:** this file is the only place progress is recorded.

## How to use this file

1. **Pick a todo** whose `needs` are all ticked. In OpenCode, run `/next-todo` to have it picked for you, or `/do-todo M3.4` to name one.
2. **Skip `[human]` todos.** They need a person, for example to supply sample files, make a judgment call, or run a build that takes hours. Agents stop and report when they reach one.
3. **One todo is one branch and one commit (or a small PR):** branch `sla/<ID>-<slug>`. Tick the box **in the same commit** as the work, and add a short result note after it, for example `→ 4f2a1c9, 312 tests pass`.
4. **If a todo turns out bigger than one session,** don't half-finish it. Split it into sub-todos (`M3.4a`, `M3.4b`) in this file, commit the split, then do the first one.
5. **If you're blocked,** leave the box unticked and add a line `  Blocked: <reason>` under the todo.

Sizes: **S** is under an hour of agent work. **M** is one session. **L** must be split before starting.

Milestones are ordered by value but can overlap. Anything whose `needs` are met can start.

---

- [ ] **M0.13** Benchmark harness that writes a metrics JSON (PLAN A6). · M · needs M0.11a, M0.12
  Done when: two runs on the same commit give identical layer hashes, and `doc/sla-fork/baseline.json` is committed.
  Result: hidden test `[benchmark]` in `tests/sla_print/sla_benchmark_tests.cpp` runs the support tool and a full SLAPrint slice per model and writes a stable-key-order JSON. `SLA_BENCH_DIR` names the corpus folder; a `manifest.yaml` in it decides which models run and the JSON names each of them by `id` and `category` only, never by file name or path; without a manifest the folder is scanned as before. `SLA_BENCH_FILTER` narrows the run to a comma separated list of ids and categories, `SLA_BENCH_OUT` names the file. Per model it records triangle count, support point count, support tree triangles and volume, pad volume, layer count, a FNV-1a `layer_hash` of the sliced layers, islands and islands without a support point, the time per phase and the peak working set. A model that throws records `error` and the run continues, and the start and the end of every model are logged with its timing. `doc/sla-fork/tools/bench_diff.py` keys the models on the id and flags a changed `layer_hash`.
   Progress 2026-09-30: manifest reading, `layer_hash`, `SLA_BENCH_FILTER`, the per model logging and the diff tool's id keying are in. Not built or run in this session, so `doc/sla-fork/baseline.json` is still missing and the todo stays unticked until the maintainer produces it from a run of the built binary.

    Result: six merges' worth of mismatches between a declaration and its use, every one read against the real signature. `CavityDetection.cpp`: the centroid returned an Eigen expression in one arm of a conditional operator (C2446), so it is an `if` returning `Vec2d(sum / total)`; `overlaps()` and `union_ex()` take ExPolygons, so a single hole of a layer is wrapped in `ExPolygons{holes[h]}`, the group region starts as `{holes[group.holes.front()]}` and the rest of the group is walked by index (the old `std::next(begin, end)` passed an iterator as the distance). `HollowingLattice.hpp` includes `admesh/stl.h`: `indexed_triangle_set` is a global-namespace type, the header used it without including it, and both of its declarations were lost, which is what the three errors in the .cpp were. `SupportFacetPaint.cpp` and the facet paint test spell it `Domain::indexed_triangle_set`, which does not exist (only `Domain::indexed_triangle_set_with_color` does). `LayerStats.cpp` and `sla_layer_stats_tests.cpp` read `perimeter_coefficient_n_per_mm2`; the field is `perimeter_coefficient_n_per_mm`, the term being N/mm over a length. `SupportPointGenerator.cpp` passed `p.cast<double()`, an Eigen expression, to `squared_distance_to_indexed_lines`, which deduces one `VectorType` from the point and from the hit point, so the two disagreed (C2672); it binds a `Vec2d` first, as the two calls next to it do. `SLAPrintSteps.cpp`: `facet_paint_of()` is a free function and `m_model_height_levels` is private, so the heights arrive as an argument from the friend that calls it, as `slice_volumes()` takes the slice grid; two `model_object()->name()` calls read the field as a function. NOT BUILT (a human builds and runs the tests).

- [x] **M0.14c** The MSVC Release build of `sla/batch-m15` still fails in libslic3r with C2672 from the forwarding `add_pillar` in `SupportTreeBuilder.hpp`: the calls at `SLA/SupportTreeUtils.hpp:666` and `SLA/SupportTreeUtilsLegacy.hpp:228` pass five arguments (endpoint, length, start radius, end radius, `StemGeometry`) and `Pillar` has no constructor that takes one. Fix it, no behaviour change. · S · needs M0.14b
    Result: `Pillar` got the constructor `(endp, h, start_radius, end_radius, stem)`, which stores the stem and tapers the given end radius with `stem.end_radius()` - exactly what the `add_pillar` overload with the same five arguments does by hand, and what `add_pillar(headid, length)` does for a pillar hanging from a head, so the forwarded call now builds the pillar that overload was written to build and the mesh does not change. The overload set is why it failed: the forwarding template `add_pillar(Args&&...)` binds its arguments as references, which is a better match for lvalues than the overload's `const Vec3d&` and by-value parameters, so the five argument call went to the template and from there into a `Pillar` constructor that did not exist. Read over every other call of the builder for the same kind of mismatch, none is broken: `add_pillar(headid, length)` and `add_pillar(pillar)` (a copy) are the two argument and copy forms, `add_pillar_base(pid, height, radius, shape)` matches its declaration, `add_head` and `add_anchor` are called with a `Head` to copy and with the six arguments of the `Head` constructor, `add_junction`, `add_bridge`, `add_crossbridge` and `add_diffbridge` are called with the two, three and four arguments their constructors take, and the `Pedestal` of `add_pillar_base` is built from five. NOT BUILT (a human builds and runs the tests).

Fix them, no behaviour change. · S · needs M0.14d
    Result: every error read against the real declaration, and none of the fixes changes what the code does. Three are the same mistake: a type the file only forward declared. `PaintOnSupportsDialog.cpp:228` calls `set_visible()` on the separator the constructor stores, so it includes `App/Yoga/Separator.hpp` (the header forward declares it for the pointer). `RotationDialog.cpp` calls `job_manager()` in five places (lines 114, 212, 222, 357 and 413) and the class was only declared, so its member templates could not be instantiated (C2027); it includes `Biz/Platform/JobManager/JobManager.hpp`, which brings `Job.hpp` and `ProgressTracker` with it. `ThumbnailImageGenerator.cpp:210` hands the camera of the fixture view to `set_fixture_view_camera()`, so it includes `App/Platform/CameraSynchData.hpp`, the definition of a struct every header of the view only forward declares. `SlaSupportPreviewService.hpp:193` is `Slic3r::sla::SupportToolModelMesh` (C2872): the fully qualified name is the one line 214 uses for `SupportToolTree` and the one the preview test uses. The three unqualified uses in the same file's `.cpp` (lines 329, 487 and 526) are qualified the same way, beside the two that already were. `JobManager.hpp:169/170` is the SLA archive import: the job is created with one argument, the paths, while `import_sla_archives_local` took four, a `const std::vector<path>&` and a `SlaArchiveRead` the job cannot pass, so `get_args_tuple` asserted that the arguments it was given plus the progress tracker are the ones the function takes. The job function now takes exactly what its job is created with, a stop token, a progress tracker and the paths, and the job is created with a copy of the paths, so it owns them on its worker thread: the vector they are read from belongs to the caller, which is gone long before the job ends, and a job that is given an lvalue keeps a reference to it. The reader seam is untouched: `read_sla_archives()` still takes the replaceable `SlaArchiveRead` the archive tests drive. `SidebarSlaSummary.cpp:551` wrote into a `const std::ostringstream`. `FileLoadingLogic.cpp:1343` handed the temporary `path.filename().string()` to `fmt::make_format_args`, which takes lvalue references (C2664); the file name is a local now and the call is `fmt::format(fmt::runtime(_u8L(...)), file_name)`, the idiom the rest of the layer uses for a translated format string, the same message and the same translation entry. `DrainHoleSuggestion.cpp:117` named `mesh_to_mesh`, which is declared nowhere: the transform of the instance is `mesh_to_world`, and the inverse transpose of its linear part is what carries the facet normal of the raycast into the frame of the layers, so the sign the hole is cut with is the one the comment already describes. Read over the tests of the touched files: `SlaArchiveImportJobTests` drives `read_sla_archives()` with its own reader and does not reach the job function, `DrainHoleSuggestionTests` has an instance that is scaled by two and moved onto the plate whose normal comes out of the inverse transpose as (0, 0, 1), and `SidebarSlaSummaryTests` expects `5:01` and `36:00` out of the stream that is no longer const. NOT BUILT (a human builds and runs the tests).

Past 16 mm from the origin that float no longer holds the scaled integer exactly, so a wall built there and the face tessellated next to it end up a float apart at the same point, `its_merge_vertices()` does not weld them, and every edge between them counts as used once. The wall of every hole of a raft (`Pad.cpp`, `straight_walls()`) and every wall of an inner part is such a strip, so the ring - a hole of a 10.5 mm radius with connector sticks on it, all of it past 16 mm from the origin - came out open along its hole, while the other footprints passed: their hole outlines are axis aligned, and a scaled coordinate on an axis is a round number that the float holds exactly. NOT BUILT (a human builds and runs the tests).

    Result: `Ring::inc()` in `TriangulateWall.hpp` wrapped `nextidx` to `startidx` and not to the beginning of the ring - the `if (nextidx != startidx) nextidx++;` guard kept it at the start instead of letting it come back to the first point - so `is_finished()` (`nextidx == idx`) declared a ring finished after `size - turn` steps as soon as the walk had come round to its own start. `synchronize_rings()` turns the second ring of a wall so that the two start at corresponding points, and a turned ring therefore stopped before the stretch of the ring in front of its start was walked: the band was not built there, and every edge of that stretch was used by the face at its end alone. A bevelled raft is where it shows, because the bevel of a rounded outline is the one wall of a raft whose two rings come from two different offsets and so start where they please: the wall between two rings that are the same polygon (the straight wall of the raft, and the wall below the bevel at a 90 degree slope) is turned by zero and walks exactly as it did before. Both indices wrap to the beginning of the ring now and the walk is over once it has come a full round, so the band is built over the whole of both rings and the seam it shares with the wall above and below it closes. No expectation of any test changed and the outline the taper test measures is the same. NOT BUILT (a human builds and runs the tests).
  - [x] **M0.14-FixHollowInterior** Four hollowing cases get an empty interior: `sla_hollowing_tests.cpp` (238) and `sla_hollowing_lattice_tests.cpp` (121) both hollow a model the easy way and are handed a mesh with no triangles in it. `generate_interior()` asks the voxel grid for two isosurfaces and never for its zero crossing, so both of them have to land inside the narrow band of the grid they are read from. Find why and fix the root cause, not the expectation. · M · needs M0.14
    Result: by OpenCode, NOT BUILT (a human builds and runs the tests). The interior was empty because `generate_interior()` (`SLA/Hollowing.cpp`) never asked for a level it could rely on. It rebuilt the distance field at `-(offset + D)` and then took the mesh at `iso_surface = D` voxels out of that grid, and the grid in between was widened by `1.1 * ceil(D)` voxels only, so the extraction level had to land in the last tenth of the band. M4.7a turned those levels from millimetres into voxel counts, which multiplied both by the voxel scale (2 to 8), and the level left the band altogether: `volumeToMesh()` has no value to cross and returns no triangle, so every case that hollows a model got an empty interior, and the product logged "Hollowed interior is empty!" and printed the part solid. The other side of the same margin was just as thin: the interior band of the mesh is `1.1 * (offset + D)` voxels wide and the rebuild needs its seed band `narrowb` voxels on both sides of its level, so the accuracy case with a `closing_distance` of 0 asked it for a level 0.35 voxels inside a band that reaches 0.35 voxels. The surface is now always taken off the zero crossing of the grid, which is the one level every grid holds inside its own band. With a closing distance the offset surface is rebuilt at `-(offset + D)`, the closing dilation widens both sides of that band by `1.1 * ceil(D) + narrowb` voxels, and the level the closing leaves behind is rebuilt once more so that `grid_to_mesh()` is asked for 0; without a closing distance the offset surface itself is the zero crossing of one rebuild. The geometry is the geometry the code had before, so the wall is still `min_thickness - 1.1 * ceil(closing_distance in voxels)` voxels, which is what `sla_hollowing_tests.cpp` allows for with the closing distance in its tolerance. Both bands are one `narrowb` wider than before (2 voxels, and `Interior::full_narrowb` reports it), which is also the only part of the grid that answers the distance queries of `remove_inside_triangles()` and `create_exclude_mask()`, and `Interior::iso_surface` is 0 now, which is where the surface of the interior mesh is. Both test files report the size of the interior and the voxel it was generated at before the check that fails on an empty one, so a run that still comes back empty says what the generator did.

  - [x] **M0.14-FixHollowInteriorBounds** The grid lattice case of `tests/sla_print/sla_hollowing_lattice_tests.cpp` asks a bundle of columns to fill the box it was built in on all three axes (443, 444), and the bundle stands 1.6mm inside the box on the two axes the columns cross. Decide whether the product or the test is wrong and fix that one. · S · needs M0.14
    Result: by OpenCode, NOT BUILT (a human builds and runs the tests). The columns are right and the expectation is wrong. `strut_axes()` puts as many axes at the wanted spacing as fit in the interval and keeps the spacing as the gap between two neighbours, "not a distance to the end", so the outermost axes of the 10mm box at a 3mm spacing stand at 4mm and 10mm, a whole spacing inside the ends, and a 0.8mm strut on them reaches from 3.6mm to 10.4mm, which is what the case measured. Only the axis the columns run along is filled from end to end, and that is what the first section of the same case pins ("Three axes at 3mm in a 10mm interval, symmetric about its middle and a whole spacing away from the ends"). The case checks that now: the bundle runs the whole height of the box along the axis of the columns, and on the two axes it crosses it stands half a strut outside the axes the engine reports and inside the box. The number of triangles, the axes and the strut widths it checked before are kept.
  - [x] **M0.14-FixTreeDeadlock** `DefaultSupports::FloorSupportsDoNotPierceModel` of `tests/sla_print/sla_print_tests.cpp` throws `std::system_error` "resource deadlock would occur" in some runs and passes in the next: a thread locks a `std::mutex` it already holds. The lock is the one of the one process wide NLopt generator (`detail::nlopt_rng_lock()`), which `NLoptOpt::optimize()` holds from the seeding to the end of `nlopt_optimize()` and whose objective of the support tree runs a TBB loop. Find the lock that is held across a parallel call and fix it, keeping the searches repeatable. · S · needs M0.14
    Result: by OpenCode, NOT BUILT (a human builds and runs the tests). The lock is `detail::nlopt_rng_lock()` (`Slic3r/Biz/Algorithms/Optimize/NLoptOptimizer.hpp`), a plain `std::mutex` which M4.5c put around `nlopt_optimize()` so that one seeded search is not fed by the numbers of another. It is held from the seeding to the end of the search, and the search runs the objective, and the objective of every search of the support tree is a model query: `pinhead_mesh_hit()` and `beam_mesh_hit()` are `execution::for_each` loops over the 16 samples of a ring under the TBB policy (`SupportTreeUtils.hpp:167, 236`), which `optimize_pinhead_placement()`, `search_widening_path()`, `check_ground_route()`, `deepsearch_ground_connection()` and `optimize_anchor_placement()` all reach. So the lock was held across a parallel loop, and that is what throws: a TBB thread which waits for a parallel loop is looking for work and can be handed any task of its arena, including a task of the loop the support tree itself is running. `add_pinheads()` runs a genetic search per support point inside an `execution::for_each` and `routing_to_model()` runs `search_ground_route()` per model facing head inside one, so a task of either loop starts a search of its own; handed to the thread that already owns the lock, it asks for that lock a second time and MSVC's `std::mutex` refuses that with `resource deadlock would occur` (EDEADLK), which the case reports as an unexpected exception. Which task goes to which thread is the scheduler, so one run passed and the next threw, and since 35d98c1a15 (M0.14r) no default tree aborts before its first step, so this path really runs now. The other locks of the support tree are not this one and are left as they are: `PillarIndex::m_mutex` and `DefaultSupportTree::m_bridge_mutex` are `BlockingMutex<ExecutionTBB>` (so also a `std::mutex`) but each is held only around an insert, a query, a copy of the index, or the builder calls of `connect_to_nearpillar()`, and none of those runs a loop or takes a `std::mutex` again (`SupportTreeBuilder`'s own `m_mutex` is a `tbb::spin_mutex` and is never taken twice on one path), and the `std::mutex` of `get_small_parts()` is held over one `push_back`. The fix keeps no lock across a parallel call: a loop started while a search owns the generator runs on the calling thread. `Execution.hpp` gains a `SequentialRegion` guard with `in_sequential_region()` (a thread local nesting count, so it belongs to the thread that raised it), `Traits<ExecutionTBB>::for_each` and `::reduce` walk the range themselves when that is set, and `NLoptOpt::optimize()` raises the guard for exactly as long as it holds `nlopt_rng_lock()`. A thread inside a search therefore never waits, is never handed a task, and can never start a second search and ask for the lock again. What is given up is the ray casts of one beam running beside each other inside a search, which searches were one at a time anyway because of that lock; the sentence of the M4.5c note that said so is corrected, and the searches are as repeatable as they were. Nothing else moved: a loop walks the same range in the same order, so every score of every search is bit for bit the score it was, loops outside a search are untouched and as parallel as they were, and no expectation of any test changed. Three sections of a new case in `tests/sla_print/sla_supptreeutils_tests.cpp` pin it: the loop of the objective runs on the thread which started the search, the guard is gone once the search is, and a search in every task of a parallel loop runs to the end and all of them return the same score for the same input, which is the repeatability the lock is there for.

    Result: by OpenCode, NOT BUILT (a human builds and runs the tests). Test code only, no product line moved. `contact_tolerance()` reads the head front radius of the configuration the case builds its tree with (`support_head_front_diameter / 2`, the very value `make_support_cfg()` puts into `SupportTreeConfig::head_front_radius_mm`) and adds the 0.05 mm a float head mesh is worth, so both contact sections ask for 0.25 mm where they asked for 0.05 and the 0.195 mm they measured sits inside it; the comment above the helper says why the point of a tree that is on its own model stands a radius off the tree rather than on it, and the comment above the case was corrected to say the same. The offset section keeps drawing the tree with the plate offset applied a second time (that is the regression it pins) and keeps the `node_trafo` check of the placement; only the two distance checks move, and they become one: the miss has to be more than three contact tolerances (0.75 mm, the measured 1.41 mm), because the nearest surface of the translated tree to a point on the model is not the translated tip, so the miss is not the length of the offset and never was. The INFO of that section prints the miss, the offset and the tolerance. Everything else in the file is unchanged, and `catch_approx.hpp` went with the only `Catch::Approx` of the file (the `isApprox` left is Eigen's).
  - [x] **M0.14-BuildFix17** `sla_print_tests` fails to compile in two test files: `tests/sla_print/sla_raft_auto_tests.cpp` (TEST_CASE "auto_raft_decision finds the cup of a cup standing open side down") uses unqualified `sla::`, `SLA::`, `Domain::` and bare `ExPolygons`; `tests/sla_print/sla_support_anchor_tests.cpp` calls `sort_points_by_zyx` before its definition. Fix both: fully qualify every name in the raft test and add missing includes; move the helper up before its first use in the anchor test. · S · needs M0.14
    Result: `sla_raft_auto_tests.cpp` adds `Slic3r/Domain/ExPolygon.hpp` and `Slic3r/Biz/Algorithms/Scaling.hpp` includes; every `sla::` → `Slic3r::sla::`, `SLA::` → `Slic3r::SLA::`, `Domain::` → `Slic3r::Domain::`, bare `ExPolygons` → `Slic3r::Domain::ExPolygons` in the diagnostic test case. `sla_support_anchor_tests.cpp` moves `sort_points_by_zyx` with its doc comment into the first anonymous namespace before the TEST_CASE that calls it. NOT BUILT (a human builds and runs the tests).
  - [x] **M0.14-FixBranchingCfg** The branching support tree reads `branchingsupport_small_pillar_diameter_percent` as `double` but the key is defined as `Percentage`, causing `std::bad_variant_access` and an empty tree. Fix the read to match the Default case and the config definition. · S · needs M0.14
    Result: product bug in `SLAPrint.cpp:make_support_cfg()` Branching case (line ~278); read now uses `c.get<Percentage>(...).get_abs_value(1.0) * pillar_r` like the Default case. Every other `c.get<T>("branchingsupport_...")` in the Branching case matches its definition in `ConfigDefsSLA.cpp`. This bug made every branching tree fail in the app too.

  - [x] **M0.15** Audit the product code for the views that outlive the mesh they look at. `Slic3r::AABBMesh` (`src/slic3r-biz-algorithms/include/Slic3r/Biz/Algorithms/AABBMesh.hpp`) stores a `const indexed_triangle_set*` and dereferences it on every query (`vertices()`, `indices()`, `normal_by_face_id()`, `query_ray_hit()`, `squared_distance()`), and its copy constructor and its assignment copy that pointer unchanged, so an `AABBMesh` built over a temporary, or one that outlives a move, a reassignment or the release of the mesh it was built from, queries memory nobody owns. `sla::SupportableMesh` (`SLA/SupportTree.hpp`) embeds one, and the gizmos and the caches of the app hold several more. Find every construction and copy of `AABBMesh`, `SupportableMesh` and the other non-owning views (`const indexed_triangle_set*` and `&` members, `Eigen::Map` over a vector that can reallocate, spans over containers that change) in src/libslic3r, src/slic3r-biz-algorithms and src/slic3r-shared, check the lifetime of what each one points at including after a move or a copy of its owner, and fix every real case. - M - needs M0.14
    Result: by OpenCode, NOT BUILT (a human builds and runs the tests). Six sites fixed, all of them the same defect wearing a different hat. (1) `SLAPrint::Steps::generate_preview()` drops `po.m_supportable_mesh` at the one place where it replaces `po.m_preview` and with it the mesh the supportable mesh's AABBMesh views; the three steps that regenerate a preview (`mesh_assembly`, `hollow_model`, `drill_holes`) each reset it before calling in, so nothing reads released memory today, but the whole safety of the site rested on three callers remembering, and a fourth caller would not have. (2) `Scene::TriangleMesh` had a defaulted move constructor that moved its `indexed_triangle_set` and its `unique_ptr<AABBMesh>` without re-pointing the view, so a moved-to object raycast the source's now-empty vectors and dangled once the source died; nothing moved one (the manager holds `unique_ptr` and every construction is in place), so copy and move are deleted rather than fixed. (3) `CutGizmo::m_solid_meshes` built an `AABBMesh` over `volume->mesh().its` and cached it until the tool was closed, while the mesh is owned by the `ModelVolume` and an undo or a volume removal destroys it; the entry now co-owns the mesh through `volume->mesh_ptr()`, which is what `CutPartSelection::Part` already did in the same tool, so nothing is copied and no groove validation can read a freed mesh. (4) `SlaSupportPointsGizmo::m_paintable_volumes` and `SlaHollowGizmo::m_paintable_volumes` hold `ModelVolume&` and an `AABBMesh` into the model, and neither gizmo heard about an undo: `SceneInteractor::set_state` moves a whole new model in and `set_object_selection` returns early when the restored selection is the same one, so no listener fires and the list keeps the volumes of a model that no longer exists (plain Ctrl+Z with the tool open). Both now take `Biz::Scene::ISceneChangedListener` and rebuild the list in `on_model_reloaded`. (5) `BedTracking`'s cache of a bed contour's AABBMesh is keyed by bed id alone, and `BedContainer::copy()` keeps the ids of the beds it copies (which is what the copy constructor of a project does), so an entry could be handed out for a bed that was freed or for the other bed of the same id; the entry now names the bed it was built from and is rebuilt when the id maps to another one. (6) `FdmViewer::m_aabb` was a `std::pair<TriangleMesh, AABBMesh>` built as `AABBMesh(Domain::TriangleMesh())`, so from the end of the constructor until `load()` rebuilt it the acceleration structure pointed at a temporary that was already freed; the mesh and the structure are one object now (`AabbMeshBox`), built in that order. Two new test files: `TriangleMeshManagerTests.cpp` pins the non-copyable, non-movable invariant and checks that the structure still answers on the mesh the manager holds, `BedTrackingTests.cpp` asks one bed and then another bed with the same id and a different contour, which is what a copied project gives, and requires each of them to be answered from its own contour. The rest of the audit (roughly forty sites) is in the body of the commit; no `Eigen::Map` and no span over a mesh exists in the three trees, and every other construction and copy is safe with the reason recorded there. No threshold, expectation, model, geometry or measurement changed, and nothing outside the lifetime of the views named above was touched.

  - [x] **M1.11c** An estimated print time in the SLA sidebar summary, like Chitubox and Lychee show, and in the archive headers that have a print time field. · M · needs M1.11a
    Result: one function, `Domain::sla_estimate_print_time()` in the new `SLA/PrintTime.{hpp,cpp}`, estimates a printer without a tilt: per layer the exposure (bottom, the linear fade over the transition layers the engine already uses, the M2.14b3 raft interface band, normal), the three `wait_*` light-off delays and the lift and the retract at their speeds, including the `bottom_*` set on the burn-in and the `*_2` second stage; summed over the layers. A term with no speed is 0 instead of a division, so a partial config view is safe. `SLAResultData` grew two fields (the hotspot edit is seven lines): the total in `print_time_s`, empty rather than 0 when there is nothing to estimate, and each layer's own time in `layer_print_times_s`; `merge_slices_and_eval_stats()` fills them. `SidebarSlaSummary` shows it as `Print time` under the layer count, `hh:mm` (hours without a leading zero, minutes always two, seconds dropped) and the en dash where there is no value, next to the existing `format_print_time()` tested with the other formatters. The formula, and the two models it sits next to, are written up in `doc/sla-fork/profiling/print-time.md`. The four non-SL1 writers each summed their own terms and did not agree: pm5/pwmx/pm5s/pm7 charged every layer the normal lift and one delay, goo and ctb charged no separation at all, and none counted the second stage; all five now write the shared estimate, and `pm5.md` and `ctb.md` say so on their print time rows. That changes the value those headers carry, so the two `.ctb` expectations were re-spelt over the new terms and the pm5 HEADER +68 field, which nothing read back before, is checked now. 10 domain cases (the hand-built 3-layer bottom+transition+normal case, the fade ramp, the interface band, the two-stage lift on both the normal and the burn-in values, and the not-an-estimate states) plus the writer and formatter cases. The `.sl1`/`.sl1s` export deliberately keeps the SL1 tilt model, so on a Prusa machine the sidebar figure and the `printTime` in `config.ini` are two estimates of the same print; picking one for both is left open. NOT BUILT (a human builds and runs the tests in this session).
  - [x] **M1.11d** The resin each model of a plate cures on its own, the way Chitubox and Lychee show it: a secondary figure in the objects list and a per model table in the sidebar summary. · M · needs M1.11a
    Result: one pure function, `SLA::object_resin_use()` in the new `SLA/ObjectResinUse.{hpp,cpp}`, sums the per object slices the result already has into the resin of every model object, and `merge_slices_and_eval_stats()` fills it: the layer loop that merges the plate now keeps the slices per object beside the merged ones (the same `get_all_polygons()` placement, extracted into `place_polygons()` so the raft outline can be placed the same way), and the raft is sliced on its own once per object on the grid the supports were sliced on, because it is part of the support slices and two models that stand close enough share one. THE RAFT RULE: the raft of the print is counted once and split between the models by FOOTPRINT, the widest layer of their bodies, which is what decides how much raft the generator grows around them; asking each model how much raft it sits on would count a shared raft twice and the models would no longer add up to the print. The bodies are counted where no other body covers the same resin and the support trees where no body stands on them, exactly as the plate totals are counted, so the models add up to the figures above them within rounding. `SLAResultData` grew one field for it (the hotspot edit is six lines). The app side is `App/SlaObjectUseRows.{hpp,cpp}` next to the M1.11b issue rows: the ml and the cost of a row come from the same `ResinEconomics` the plate figure uses, so a row cannot disagree with the figure it breaks down, and the cost carries no currency symbol because no config key says which currency a resin is sold in (`bottle_cost` is a plain money amount, so the plate cost is shown the same way). The sidebar summary shows the rows under a `Per model (N)` toggle, but only with more than one model on the plate, and before the first slice it lists the models of the bed with the en dash the plate figures carry in that state; the objects list, which is a table of icons with no room for a second line, shows the same figures as a tooltip on the row of the model. Both are SLA only, like the whole window. 11 cases in `tests/sla_print/sla_object_resin_use_tests.cpp` (two cubes of different size in the ratio of their volumes, the total equal to the sum, a body standing on another model counted once, the support tree around a body, the raft of a shared pad split by footprint in three ways, the widest layer as the footprint, the entries in any order) and 12 in the app suite for the rows, the text and the en dash. NOT BUILT: a human build and run is needed before this can be called green. Merge with sla/batch-m15 (M4.8h suggest hollowing, and its build fix) 2026-09-30: both features kept. The layer loop places the per object slices once and hands the same placed polygons to both tables, so the per object stats of M4.8h and the resin per object of M1.11d share the one pass over them; one index of the print objects numbers both the M4.8h accumulators and the M1.11d raft slices, and the heights of the raft slicing and the model name follow the build fix (a field, not a call).

Add an Inkscape 1.4 backend and pick it automatically when cairosvg cannot be imported: find the executable through `INKSCAPE` or `shutil.which("inkscape")`, rasterise each needed size with `inkscape <svg> --export-type=png --export-width=<w> --export-height=<h> --export-filename=<png>` into a temporary folder inside the output tree, build the .ico/.icns/.jpg with Pillow from the same code the cairosvg path uses, add `--backend {auto,cairosvg,inkscape}` and `--dry-run`. · M · needs —
    Result: `--backend auto` (the default) uses cairosvg when it imports and Inkscape otherwise; `--backend inkscape` finds the executable through `$INKSCAPE` (a full path or a bare name) and then `shutil.which("inkscape")`, and when neither resolves it exits 2 naming Settings > Apps > Advanced app settings > App execution aliases > Inkscape, which is what the Microsoft Store build needs. The only thing the two backends do differently is `Rasteriser.png(svg, w, h)`, which returns PNG bytes; the .ico, .icns, .png and .jpg are all still built by the same Pillow functions, so nothing is duplicated. Inkscape has no multi-size export, so it runs once per size, writing into a `.render_icons-*/` folder beside the outputs rather than %TEMP% and deleting it in a `finally`, on success and on failure alike; a run killed outright can only leave a folder the .gitignore now keeps out of the repository. It also has a 120 s per-export timeout, because a hung first run is otherwise indistinguishable from a slow one. `--dry-run` (with `--check` kept as an alias) lists the sources, the files it would write and the backend, and reports a missing backend instead of failing, so the choice is visible before anything is written. Checked with a stub `inkscape` and a stub `$INKSCAPE`, off to one side: the four outputs are written and the scratch folder is gone, and a non-zero exit, an export that writes no file, a timeout and an `INKSCAPE` that names a non-executable are each reported with the exit code and Inkscape's last line. Nothing in `resources/icons/` was rendered and no binary was committed, so M1.12 stays unticked until the tool is run for real.

- [x] **M2.5b** The sign of the normal a drain hole carries. M4.8f left a note that the M2.5 drain hole gizmo may store the normal its raycast returns unnegated, which is the opposite of what the cutter needs, so a hole placed by click would be cut out of the model instead of into it. Establish the convention from the engine and from the legacy `GLGizmoHollow`, fix whichever of the gizmo, the engine and the M4.8f suggestion disagrees with it, keep the 3MF files of upstream PrusaSlicer cutting the same way, and test it. · S · needs M2.5
  Result: the convention is the one the engine cuts with, and the one the legacy `GLGizmoHollow` already used: the normal points INTO the material. `sla::to_mesh` builds the cutter as a closed cylinder that sits on the position and runs along the normal, `sla::transform_drainhole_points` pulls its near cap 1mm back along that same direction so the boolean stays well defined, and `create_exclude_mask` reads the axis as `pos + normal * height`, so all three say the same thing. Of the three producers the M4.8f `suggest_drain_hole()` was already right (it decides by the direction of its search rather than by the winding) and the legacy gizmo was right (it stored `-raycast_normal`); the M2.5 gizmo was the wrong one, so a hole placed by click, or dragged to a new spot, was stored with the sign the cutter cannot use. `SlaHollowGizmo::hit_to_object_pos_normal` now ends in a new pure `drain_hole_pos_normal_in_object_mesh()` in `SlaDrainHolesEditing.{hpp,cpp}`, shared with the editor, which transforms the position, carries the normal over with the same inverse transpose as before (so a rotated or non-uniformly scaled volume is unaffected) and negates it, so the gizmo cannot get the sign wrong a second time; its degenerate-normal fallback of +Z follows the same sign. The hole marker needed nothing: it was already drawn from the position along `+normal` like the cutter, so it now matches what is actually cut. Nothing on disk moved: the 3MF writer and reader are untouched, so files written by upstream PrusaSlicer, which stores the negated normal, still load and cut the same way. One engine test in `tests/sla_print/sla_hollowing_tests.cpp`: a click on the bottom of the 20mm cube comes out of the gizmo's own computation at (12, 9, 0) pointing +Z, that hole goes through `transform_drainhole_points` and `sla::hollow_mesh_and_drill` into the hollowed cube, and a ray straight down its axis out through the floor meets no surface of the drilled shell, where the same ray meets the floor of the undrilled copy of that shell (the control, so an empty mesh cannot pass it). NOT BUILT, a human build and run is needed. To check in a running app later: click a hole on a flat bottom and slice, the hole has to come out on the underside.

    - [x] **M2.14b2a** The pattern itself: a `raft_infill` choice (none, grid, honeycomb) with the size of one cell and the wall between two cells, cut out of the inside of the raft by the pad generator while a solid skin under the top face and a solid rim around the pattern are kept, so the object never rests on a hole. Default none, so a raft stays the solid slab of today, bit for bit. · M · needs M2.14b1
      Result: four keys in the Raft group under `raft_edge_taper` (orders 6-9, the six order values below them moved up by four): `raft_infill` (None, Grid, Honeycomb, default None), `raft_infill_spacing` (clear size of one cell, 2.0 mm), `raft_infill_wall` (material between two cells, which is also the width of the rim, 0.4 mm) and `raft_infill_skin` (solid material left under the top face, 0.5 mm). All four are in `raft_shape_settings()`, so they show next to the other raft knobs for every raft type that prints a raft and None hides them. The value travels like the other shared knobs: `raft_infill()` in `SLAPrint.cpp` reads the keys into a `Domain::SLA::RaftInfill` (a config saved before they existed gets the solid raft), `raft_preset_to_pad_values` passes it through (no raft type replaces the pattern, that is a tuning question against Lychee and Chitubox like `SKATE_BRIM_FACTOR`) and `make_pad_cfg` sets `sla::PadConfig::infill`; all four keys invalidate the pad step. In the generator, `cut_infill_cells` pulls the outline of a part in by the infill wall to get the solid rim, `infill_cells` lays a lattice of open cells over what is left of it (squares for Grid, hexagons of a hexagonal lattice for Honeycomb, with the wall between two neighbours so that what is left between the cells is the ribs of the pattern) and clips the cells to the rim; the cells run from the bottom face of the raft up to the top of the skin, which is the raft height minus the skin or, in a raft with a cavity, the floor of the cavity the object sits in, so no cell ever reaches under the object. What is left of the interior around the cells gets the walls and the flat ceiling that closes them, and the bottom face becomes the rim ring plus those ribs, so the walls of the cells stand on its edges: the raft is still one closed solid, with a through hole pattern in the layers of the cells and a solid slab in the layers of the skin. A pattern that would need more than 4096 cells, an interior with no cell in it and a skin that swallows the raft are all printed solid instead, because the alternatives are a hole too small to print and a closed pocket no printer can fill. `tests/sla_print/sla_raft_infill_tests.cpp`: the keys reach the pad config and a config without them gets None; None is the same mesh as the default (same index count, same volume, no hole anywhere in it); Grid and Honeycomb give the same bounding box, a smaller volume and a layer inside the cells with less resin in it and holes in it; in the skin the filled raft has as much material as a solid one and no hole at all. `sla_raft_preset_tests.cpp`, `SlaRaftSettingsTests.cpp` and the invalidation key list extended. NOT BUILT (no build in the session).
  - [x] **M2.14b2c** The filled raft of M2.14b2a is not the mesh it was meant to be: its cells are not closed at the top and a raft with no cavity gets no skin under its top face at all. · S · needs M2.14b2a
      Result: two bugs in the infill path of `Pad.cpp`, both found by `sla_raft_infill_tests.cpp` (the filled raft held more resin than the solid one and its top layer was the solid one with a hole in it). `create_infill_geometry` triangulated the *ribs* at `infill_cells_top_z()`, which is the footprint the skin stands on and not the ceiling of a cell, so every cell was open at the top and the raft was no longer one closed solid: `its_volume` came out larger than the solid slab's and the layers through the cells sliced as the full outline instead of a ring of ribs with the cells in it. The ceiling is now triangulated over the cells, which closes each of them and is at the same time the underside of the skin above; the ribs need no face there, they are the skin. `PadConfig3D::infill_cells_top_z()` returned the *higher* of the skin top and the floor of the cavity (`z > floor_z ? z : floor_z`), so a raft with no cavity (`wall_height_mm` 0, the full plate of today) got 0: the cells ran up to the top face, there was no skin and the object would have rested on the open cells. A raft with a cavity whose floor is lower than the skin had its cells cut through the floor the object sits on. It is now `-std::max(infill.skin_mm, wing_height)`, the lower of the two, which is what its own comment has always claimed. Nothing else of M2.14b2a, M2.14b2b or M2.25 moves, so a raft with no infill is still bit for bit the raft of today. `sla_raft_infill_tests.cpp` gained the other half of the bound: a raft with a 1.5 mm cavity, where a filled raft is the solid raft between the top face and the floor of the cavity (the same ring of material with the hole the cavity is) and the pattern is open over the whole height of the slab below that floor. NOT BUILT (a human builds and runs the tests in this session).
  - [x] **M2.14b3** A separate raft interface layer: the skin between the raft and the object, printable with its own exposure and its own thickness, which today is not a layer we can address on its own. · M · needs M2.14b1
    Result: two keys in the Raft group under `raft_infill_skin` (orders 10-11, the six order values below them moved up by two), both in `raft_shape_settings()` so they show next to the other raft knobs for every raft type that prints one and None hides them: `raft_interface_thickness` ("Raft interface thickness", mm, 0-30, default 0) and `raft_interface_exposure` ("Raft interface exposure", s, 0-120, default 0). The band is decided once in the domain beside `sla_bottom_layer_count()`: `Domain::raft_interface_band(raft height, thickness, layer height)` and `Domain::sla_raft_interface(cfg, layer count)`, where the raft height is `pad_wall_height + pad_wall_thickness`, which is `PadConfig::full_height()`. The band is the top that many millimetres of the raft as whole layers; a thickness thinner than half a layer still gets the one layer it rounds to, and a raft that ends on a layer boundary is not one layer taller for it. It never reaches into the bottom layers: a band that would is cut back to them, because a layer that is both is exposed at the bottom exposure - the burn-in is what holds the raft to the build plate. Every writer that stores an exposure per layer applies the band: pm5/pwmx in the LAYERDEF entries, goo and ctb in their layer records, and all three count it in the print time they write (`RaftInterface::print_time_delta_s`); the ctb header's transition layer count grows by the band, the reading of that field that fits both the burn-in and the interface. The .sl1 and .sl1s formats hold a single exposure for the whole print, so the interface exposure has nowhere to go there, `SL1.cpp` says so at the spot, and both tooltips say so for the user. Both defaults are 0, so a print without the interface writes the file of today byte for byte. The interface changes no geometry, so both keys invalidate the export data alone (`slapsMergeSlicesAndEval`). Tests: `SlaLayerHeightTests.cpp` for the band, its layer range, the bottom-layer precedence and the print-time delta; `PM5ExportTests.cpp`, `GooExportTests.cpp` and `CtbExportTests.cpp` slice a cube with a 2 mm raft and an interface of 0.5 mm, read the layer records back and check 35 s on the 11 bottom layers, 6 s up to layer 30, 12 s on the 10 interface layers and 6 s after (ctb also checks that the transition count grows to 20 and that its print time counts the band). NOT BUILT (no build in the session).

  - [x] **M2.16b** The per point tip and stem geometry has to reach the mesh, not only the support point: the tip shape builds a cone or a ball contact, a knot ball is put at the junction, and the stem becomes a polygon prism or tapers, in the default tree and where the branching tree builds the same parts. · M · needs M2.16
    Result: the builder records carry the geometry of their support point: a new `sla::StemGeometry` (cross section 0 or 3 to 12 sides, taper in mm over the pillar, the radius it may not go below) on `Head` and `Pillar`, and `tip_shape` plus `knot_radius_mm` on `Head`. `sla::stem_geometry()` reads them off a support point (a side count outside 3 to 12 stays round, a missing or negative taper is no taper, the minimum radius is `head_fallback_radius_mm`). `pinhead()` got a shape argument: Default builds the two sphere pinhead bit for bit, Cone closes the circle the robe ends at to a point where the front sphere's pole was, Ball builds the whole sphere of the front radius; all three are as long as the default one and reach as deep, so the junction and with it the pillar do not move. `get_mesh(Head)` adds the knot as a ball at the junction. `get_mesh(Pillar)` builds a `polygon_cone` for a stem with sides (4 * sides triangles instead of 4 * steps) and a tapered pillar is a frustum down to `StemGeometry::end_radius()`, which `add_pillar` computes while it builds the pillar and clamps at the minimum radius; `add_pillar_base` ends the base where the pillar is instead of where it starts. Both trees pass it on: the default tree through the head and `create_ground_pillar`, the branching tree through `calculate_pinhead_placement` and `GroundConnection::stem`. The collision checks measure what is there: the new `Head::junction_radius()` is the knot or the back of the pinhead and is used by the ground scan of the default tree, by its ground route and by the leaves of the branching tree; all three tip shapes sink into the model equally deep, so the pinhead clearance estimate does not change. The pad is sampled from the support mesh, so it follows the pillar, and the live preview hash mixes the four fields too, since they change the geometry the preview draws. Tests: `tests/sla_print/sla_support_point_geometry_tests.cpp` (11 cases): the default tip shape builds the pinhead of before bit for bit, a cone tip is smaller and a ball tip bigger than the default one at the same length and depth, the knot grows the head by its ball at the junction, a hexagon stem carries the hexagon of a round pillar of the same circumscribed radius in 24 triangles, the taper thins the pillar towards its base and stops at the minimum radius, a point that asks for nothing keeps the old mesh, and both trees carry the point's shape, knot, sides and taper into the head, the pillar and the base. NOT BUILT (no build in this session). Still open: no UI control for the four fields and no global key.

New `sla::head_penetration()` and `sla::base_size()` read the per point contact depth and base off a support point; `calculate_pinhead_placement` and `DefaultSupportTree::add_pinheads` build the head with the point's penetration, so the junction (and with it the pillar) follows it. `deepsearch_ground_connection` takes the point the connection starts from and sizes the pedestal from it, and `GroundConnection::full_radius` carries the radius that counts as a full pillar, so `build_ground_connection` adds the base of a point whose pillar is thinner than the configured one. The branching builder remembers which support point every leaf came from (`leaf_pts`) and gives its ground connection the point's own base and radius, which also keeps a long thin pillar from being widened to the node it continues to; the default tree's mini pillar path now widens to the point's own radius (`search_widening_path` returns the radius it was asked for instead of the global one) and does not run at all for a pillar that is already the width its point asked for. Points without a per point size are unchanged. Tests: the branching tree gives the Light and Heavy points their own 2 mm and 4 mm bases, a point without a preset keeps the global one, a deeper contact depth puts the head's junction closer to the model in both trees, and a 12 mm gap under a Light point leaves its pillar 0.4 mm wide in both trees.

- [x] **M2.21** Supports stay on the model after leaving the support tool (user, 2026-09-29), and show in Prepare for every supported object. · M · needs M2.20
  Result: `SlaSupportPreviewService` owns the support tree and raft preview in Prepare: it watches the slicing input, project, build plate and selection, rebuilds changed objects on a worker thread through `SLASupportTool` (never slicing), and lifts the model node to the support elevation via `PlaterScenePresenter::set_sla_lift`. The gizmo no longer draws its own tree. Tested: both suites green 2026-09-30. Awaiting the user's check in the running app.
  - [x] **M2.21b** The support preview keeps up on the maintainer's benchmark figures (50-100 MB): a burst of edits (dragging a point, a slider moving a per point value) ends in one build of the final state, a key that changes while a build runs stops it, and a result of an older key is thrown away. · M · needs M2.21
    Result: by OpenCode, NOT BUILT (a human builds and runs the tests). The new `SlaSupportPreviewSchedule` (`SlaSupportPreviewSchedule.{hpp,cpp}`) owns the debounce and the cancellation per object, behind a two-method timer interface (`ISlaSupportPreviewTimer`, the platform one is `Biz::Platform::TimerQueue`, a test fires it by hand): every key change replaces the build waiting for the object's window (`debounce`, 200 ms, a named constant), a build already running for that object is stopped through the stop `sla::build_support_tree_for_tool` already takes, and `is_current()` compares the request's signature with the object's newest one, so a stale result is never installed. The service keeps one `Pending` per object (matrix, points, configs, elevation, bed transform) and clones the model mesh only when a build really starts, so a burst of edits of a big figure costs one clone and one tree instead of one per event; `start_next_job()` drops the queued jobs whose signature is already stale. The result still comes back through the main thread dispatcher, the main thread never waits for a build, and `build_nodes` still frees the previous tree and raft before installing the new ones, so an object never holds more than the tree it shows. Tests: `test/Slic3r/App/Plater/SlaSupportPreviewScheduleTests.cpp` with a fake timer and a fake builder: four rapid edits build once and for the last signature, every object debounces on its own, a newer edit stops the build in flight and its result is stale, another object's build keeps running, a forgotten object and a project change drop everything, and a debounce of a gone service builds nothing. Nothing slices: the meshes still come from `SLASupportTool` alone. Known gap: the mesh clone of a build is still taken on the main thread, now once per burst instead of once per event.
  - [x] **M2.21c** Starting a support preview build no longer stalls the main thread on a 50-100 MB figure: the main thread hands the worker a snapshot of the model's meshes instead of a copy of the model, and the merging, the AABB and the tree are built on the worker. · M · needs M2.21b
    Result: by OpenCode, NOT BUILT (a human builds and runs the tests). Option A, because the model already stores its geometry as immutable shared data: a `ModelVolume` holds its mesh as a `std::shared_ptr<const TriangleMesh>` and every copy of a volume shares that pointer, so the clone of M2.21b never copied a vertex either, it copied the settings, facets, points and instances around the mesh, O(model) on the main thread for every build. The new `sla::support_tool_model_mesh` is the snapshot: the MODEL PART volumes as `{shared_ptr<const TriangleMesh>, Transform3d}` parts, one shared pointer and one matrix each, no vertex and no config. `build_support_tree_for_tool` and `generate_support_points_for_tool` got an overload over that snapshot, which `build_object_mesh` (the merge, the stats), `AABBMesh`, the tree and the pad all run in, and the `ModelObject` ones now delegate to it, so the support tool gizmo is untouched. `SlaSupportPreviewService::start_due_build` takes the snapshot instead of cloning and the worker builds from it, which is the whole of the change on that side. No data race: the meshes behind the pointers are const and the model replaces them (`set_mesh`), it never rewrites one in place, which is the same guarantee the clone gave (the one in-place writer, `scale_geometry_after_creation`, is documented to run only while the meshes are not shared yet, at load time); the volume matrices and the support points are copied on the main thread as before. M2.21b is untouched: the debounce, the cancel through the worker's stop, `is_current()` and the result through the main thread dispatcher all stand, and nothing slices. Tests: `tests/sla_print/sla_support_tool_tests.cpp` (the snapshot is the volume's own mesh down to the vertex buffer and leaves out what is not a model part, a snapshot keeps the geometry the model had after its mesh and its placement changed, the tree, the raft and the generated points out of a snapshot are the ones out of the object) and two cases in `test/Slic3r/App/Plater/SlaSupportPreviewScheduleTests.cpp` with a fake builder: a burst of edits of a small model and of a 300 x 300 quad one builds once each and copies zero vertices (counted against the model's own buffers, so the check does not depend on the mesh size), and a build that started keeps the mesh it was started from while the model swaps that volume's mesh and moves it. Not checked in a running app.

    Result: by OpenCode, NOT BUILT (a human builds and runs the tests). Three object level keys next to the hollowing ones, all in the `Print_Hollowing_Hollowing` group, all with a tooltip and all at the value that keeps today's print: `hollowing_infill` (a combobox None / Grid / Cubic over the new `sla::HollowingInfillType`), `hollowing_infill_spacing` (mm, 0.5 to 30, 3) and `hollowing_infill_strut` (mm, 0.1 to 10, 0.5), each with an invalidation entry that re-runs `slaposHollowing`. `make_hollowing_infill_cfg` (SLAPrint.cpp) reads them into the new `sla::HollowingInfillConfig` the way `raft_infill` reads the raft ones, so a config without the keys is the plain cavity. The new `SLA/HollowingLattice.{hpp,cpp}` is the geometry: `hollowing_lattice_axes` puts one axis per strut spacing, symmetric about the middle of the interval and as many as fit (at most 128, the spacing stretched to spread them when the cap bites), `make_hollowing_lattice` builds square prisms of the strut width over the bounding box of the interior and returns **one mesh per strut direction** (a Grid is the columns of a square grid along Z, a Cubic those plus the same columns along X and along Y), and `subtract_lattice_from_cavity` cuts them out with `MeshBoolean::cgal::minus`, one direction at a time. A strut is never wider than nine tenths of the spacing, so the struts of one direction never touch and a bundle is a set of disjoint closed solids, which is what a CGAL boolean takes (it throws on a self intersecting input); the directions cross each other, which is why they are three booleans and not one. The shape of the change is that the struts are cut out of the *interior* and not unioned into the model: `hollow_model` calls `subtract_lattice_from_cavity` after the lossless simplification of the interior, so the one CSG difference of the hollowing step is the cavity without the struts. Everything downstream then needs no new code: the struts are printed as solid material, the 2D `slice_csgmesh_ex` of the slice step cuts them out of the layers with the same `diff_ex` it already used, the fallback path and `create_exclude_mask` see an interior like any other, and the drain holes are cut at the later `slaposDrillHoles` step, which opens a strut standing across a hole as well, so a strut cannot plug one. None skips all of it and today's hollow is today's hollow bit for bit. Tests: the new `tests/sla_print/sla_hollowing_lattice_tests.cpp` (the three keys in the hollowing group with their defaults, `make_hollowing_infill_cfg` with and without the keys, the axes and the pattern of a Grid and a Cubic, None leaving the cavity untouched, a grid adding resin with the shell still a closed solid and every strut at least 0.2mm inside the outer surface, and a drain hole through a column of the grid leaving the struts and the floor open) plus the three keys in the invalidation test. Known gaps: no UI control yet, that is M2.29b; the lattice is axis aligned, so a strut in a part that is not axis aligned is trimmed to the cavity by the boolean rather than following the part; and a strut wider than the spacing is silently narrowed to nine tenths of it.

The pattern itself is picked in one place: the new `hollowing_infill_patterns`, `hollowing_infill_of_index` and `hollowing_infill_index` of the new App header `SlaHollowingInfillSettings.hpp` are the combobox order and the enum, so the tool cannot write a pattern the user did not pick, and an index or a value that is not a pattern is the plain cavity instead of nothing. An island point is made of the shape of a part and not of a sample, so a blocker never takes it away. `generate_support_points_for_tool` and `SLAPrint::Steps::prepare_for_generate_supports` (the new `facet_paint_of`) hand the paint over, so the tool and the slice time agree, and a model with nothing painted gives an empty paint and therefore the points of the generator of today. - [x] **M2.31** Leaving the support point tool keeps the points it generated: closing the dialogue applies what is waiting, so the model keeps its supports and the preview, the lift and the slice see them. Every path that ends the tool applies the pending points, exactly as the Apply button does, undo snapshot included; Discard stays the explicit way to throw them away, and a generation that is still running is cancelled and its result dropped. · M · needs M2.1, M2.21
  Result: the generated points no longer die with the tool. `on_deactivated` reset `m_has_generated_points` and `m_generated_support_points`, so Generate followed by closing the dialogue left the ModelObject without points: `SlaSupportPreviewService` (M2.21) only previews an object whose ModelObject has points, so there was no tree and no lift, and `SLAPrint::Steps::support_points` sliced the model's own (empty) points, which is what the user reported (supports on, no points on the object). Every path out of the tool now ends in `apply_pending_points_on_leaving()`, which calls `apply_generated_points()`: `on_deactivated` (Escape, the window's close, another tool, going to Preview), the selection change onto another object, and `on_project_deactivated`, which the tool now answers the way every other gizmo does (the project view closing) by calling `on_deactivated`. The write needs the tool's project to be the selected one, since that is where `SceneInteractor::modify_sla_support_points` puts it; once the selection has moved on to another project the points are dropped, because there is no model left to put them on. The write is the one the Apply button makes: the `SlaSupportPointsApply` snapshot first, then the points on the object through `SceneInteractor::modify_sla_support_points` with `sla_points_status` AutoGenerated, so the preview service lifts the model and draws the tree, the Slice button slices the points and one Ctrl+Z takes them off again (the `DeactivateGizmo` snapshot `GizmoManager::deactivate_current_tool` takes after the tool is done sits on the state that has them, so the undo of the close is the state without). A generation that is still running is cancelled and its result dropped, so there is nothing to apply; Auto support keeps no pending points either, `on_auto_support_completed` writes every model's points as the run goes under the one snapshot of the run (M2.6b), so what it already did stays and only the rest of the queue is dropped. Discard is unchanged and stays the one way to throw the points away: it still clears them before it closes the tool, so the close finds nothing pending. The selection change also stopped calling `discard_edited_points()`, which put back the points an edit session started from; those edits are on the model already (`commit_edited_points_live`, one `SlaSupportPointsEdit` snapshot each), so they stay, which is what `end_editing()` in `on_deactivated` always did. The rule and the write are one new file, `App/Plater/SlaSupportPointsLeaving.{hpp,cpp}`: `apply_generated_points_on_leaving(has_generated_points, discard_requested)` and `apply_generated_support_points(object, points)`, now the only write of generated points (the Apply button, leaving the tool and Auto support all share it). The Apply button stays, with a tooltip saying it is optional, and the *Generate* bullet of `doc/sla-fork/user-guide/getting-started.md` was corrected to say the points are written when you leave the tool. If an edit session is open while generated points are pending, the generated points are what lands, which is what pressing Apply did as well. Tests: `SlaSupportPointsGizmoTests.cpp` (the points a generation produced land on the ModelObject with AutoGenerated, nothing is written with none pending or with a generation still running, Discard leaves an object without points and an object that had points untouched, an edit session left by deactivation keeps its edits) and `SlaSupportPointsUndoTests.cpp` (one undo removes the points leaving the tool applied, Discard plus the close write nothing undo can see). NOT BUILT (a human builds and runs the tests in this session); not checked in a running app.

  points the two were 5 mm apart, so a click tested a copy of the mesh that is not on the screen: the
  point it placed was not on the surface the user clicked, and nothing selected, dragged or removed
  where the glyphs are. The lift is now one value, the one `PlaterScenePresenter::sla_lift()` answers
  for the object, and it is asked for instead of computed: `SlaSupportPointsGizmo::refresh_tool_lift()`
  runs on activation, on every selection change, after every write of points (a click, a removal, an
  edit of the selection, Apply, Auto support, "Remove all points") and before every raycast, and
  `render_scene` re-checks it each frame, so a lift the service takes or drops mid-frame cannot leave
  the raycast behind. While the tool is open on an object the model is raised by its support
  elevation, the way Chitubox raises it while supports are edited, so there is no jump when the first
  point is added (the service lifts by the same elevation from then on) and the object falls back
  onto the plate when the tool closes and it has no points. The rule itself is pure and testable in
  the new `App/Plater/SlaSupportPointsLift.{hpp,cpp}`: `sla_support_points_lift(tool_open, scene_lift,
  support_elevation, tool_owns_lift)` answers Take / Keep / GiveBack and who holds the lift (only a
  lift the tool holds itself is given back, a lift of the service is never taken away), plus
  `sla_support_points_drawing_trafo(instance_trafo, applied_lift)` and
  `sla_support_points_hit_position(volume_trafo, hit)` for the one transform the volumes, the glyphs
  and the rectangle selection use and for the hit that becomes a point. Zero elevation still means
  the object on the plate. **The settings.** `SlaSupportPointsDialog` splits its one "Support point
  settings" block into two titled sections, "New supports" and "Selected supports (N)", each with

    drawn, how far it is from the camera), `SlaSupportTreePart` is one drawn piece of a support tree
    (the object it belongs to, the support point index, the two ends of the piece on the screen, its
    thickness, its depth), and `SlaSupportPointTarget` is what a click found (the index, and whether
    it was the marker or the tree, since a drag starts from the marker and the tree only selects).

    never under 6 px (a point far away is a pixel or two and still has to be clickable) and never over

    the marker nearest the cursor and, of two markers at the same place, the one nearer the camera,
    which is the one drawn in front. `sla_support_tree_part_at()` is the same over the pieces, testing
    the distance to the segment, so the head, the pillar and the foot of a support are all on it, with
    `sla_support_tree_pick_slack_px` (3 px) so a pillar thinner than a pixel is still clickable.
    `sla_support_point_click_target()` puts the two together the way a click reads: the marker first,

    tool to fall back to the surface of the model. The one function that needs the camera,
    `sla_support_point_screen_radius()`, measures how large a sphere is drawn at a position, so the
    radius a click follows is the size of the glyph on the screen and not the tip diameter in mm as
    before. **In the tool** `SlaSupportPointsGizmo` collects the markers and the pieces of the object
    it works on (`collect_point_markers()`, `collect_tree_parts()`, `point_at()`) and asks that one

- [x] **M2.34** The support tree drawn in Prepare touches the model at every support point (user: "The supports are also not contacting neither the model nor the support points"). The pinhead of a support point is built at the point, so the tree the preview draws has to be drawn with the same transform the model is, and neither side may add a transform of its own. · S · needs M2.21, M2.33
  Result: the tree was drawn `bed_trafo * translation(0, 0, elevation)` over a mesh the engine had already placed with the instance matrix. That matrix is a world matrix and it already carries the offset of the build plate: `BedPlacement::layout()` (called by `layout_after_project_load()` on every project load) shifts every instance by the transform of the plate it sits on, and `get_bed_trafo()` derives that transform from `bed.contour_aabb().min`, which for an SLA printer is never zero (the `bed_shape` of the SL1 begins at 1.48x1.02, the community ones at 0.5x0.5). So every tree in Prepare was drawn that far from its own model and from its own point markers: the pillar tops ended beside the points instead of on them, which is the report. The plate transform is gone. The whole placement is now one pure function of the service, `sla_support_tree_placement(instance_matrix, lift)` in `SlaSupportPreviewService.{hpp,cpp}`, which returns both halves of the one convention and is what `refresh()` hands the worker and `build_nodes()`: `object_to_world` (the instance matrix, the world placement of the object, NOT lifted, which is the contract `build_support_tree_for_tool()` documents) and `node_trafo` (the support elevation, the lift `PlaterScenePresenter::set_sla_lift()` gives the model, so `node_trafo * object_to_world` is exactly the transform on the model's own instance node and the one the tool's point markers are placed with). `build_nodes()` lost its `bed_trafo` and `elevation` parameters and the dead `ObjectPreview::elevation`, and `refresh()` no longer reads the transform of the plate at all, so there is nothing left to multiply in by mistake. The rule is written on both sides: at `sla_support_tree_placement` and at the comment of `build_support_tree_for_tool()` in `libslic3r/SLASupportTool.hpp`, which now says that the instance matrix is a world matrix that carries the plate offset and that the caller lifts by the elevation the model itself is drawn by. Nothing else of the service moved: the debounce, the cancellation, the snapshot of the model meshes, the theme colours and the render layer are as they were, and the drawn result is the tree of the slicer (the engine has always built it from the unlifted object, the caller lifts it by the elevation). Tests: a new case in `SlaSupportPreviewServiceTests.cpp` builds a model written from scratch (a plate overhanging a base on every side) with one instance that is moved and turned about Z and about X the way Auto orient leaves one, dropped onto the plate, with the offset of the build plate in its matrix, gives it four points on the underside of the overhang, then plays both parts of the preview: `build_support_tree_for_tool()` with the placement of the service, the tree drawn with the node transform of the placement and the points drawn the way the model and its markers are drawn (`sla_support_points_drawing_trafo`), and the largest distance from a point to the tree over an `AABBMesh` of the drawn tree, reported by INFO with the vector that decided it. A model raised by its supports and a model with a raft around it (zero elevation, the node transform is then the identity) both have to stay within 0.05 mm, and a third section pins the offset itself: a tree drawn with the plate transform of the SL1 applied a second time misses the points by exactly the length of that offset, so the case fails on any node transform that brings the plate back. NOT BUILT (a human builds and runs the tests in this session); NOT CHECKED in a running app.

The automatic placement sizes every point it generates per point: the lowest island of the model, the first part that prints, takes the Heavy preset, every other generated point takes a preset of its own.
