Not built or run in this session.

- [x] **M4.15** Auto orient progress is reported as a fraction, so a search that is 55 % of the way through reads as 55 % and not as 5500 %. · S · needs M4.12
  Result: the two sides count differently. `sla::AutoOrientStatus` hands out per cent, 0..100 (-1 when it only asks whether to go on), while `JobManager::ProgressTracker` carries a fraction, 0..1, which is what every reader of it multiplies by 100 (`PopNotificationCenter.cpp`, the pop notification of the search), so the 55 the engine reported arrived as 5500 % and the panel's own bar only looked right because it read the fraction as a per cent. `run_auto_orient()` in `RotationDialog.cpp` now reports `auto_orient_progress_fraction(percent)` into the tracker and reads it back with `auto_orient_progress_percent()` for the bar and its "Auto orient %1%/100" text; both live in the new `include/Slic3r/App/Plater/AutoOrientProgress.hpp`, which also clamps what the engine hands out, so the tracker's own range assert can no longer fire. Every other `ProgressTracker::set` in slic3r-shared was checked and already sent a fraction: `FileLoadingLogic.cpp` (the clamped share of the files loaded), `FileDownloaderJob.cpp` (the bytes written over the size, remapped into the download's slice of the bar), `MeshRepairJob.cpp` (the meshes done over the count, then 1.0) and `PrintHostJobManager.cpp` (the bytes sent over the total); `ArrangeInteractor.cpp` and `PresetUpdaterInteractor.cpp` are handed a tracker but only set progress details on it, and the support tool and export jobs do not report into the job manager at all. Test `test/Slic3r/App/Plater/AutoOrientProgressTests.cpp`: every per cent from 0 to 100 becomes a fraction inside 0..1 and reads back as the same per cent, out of range values clamp, and a job reporting those fractions through a real `JobManager` never leaves a value above 1 (or below 0) in the status the panel and the notification read. NOT BUILT: a human build and run is needed before this can be called green.
- [x] **M4.16** The support tool stops promptly when it is asked to: it gives up within about two seconds of its stop function turning true, wherever it is (per layer, per point batch, per tree head batch, per pad step), and the result of a run nobody stopped is bit for bit what it was. · M · needs —
    Result: the `SupportToolStop` of `generate_support_points_for_tool` and `build_support_tree_for_tool` reaches far less of the engine than it looks. The measured failure is `tests/sla_print/sla_robustness_tests.cpp` "a one metre mesh neither crashes nor hangs", case "a one metre cube": a 1000 mm cube sliced at a 100 mm layer height is ten layers of a metre wide plate, and the support stage was still running 185 s after it started although its stop function had said yes at 60 s. Five places on that path never asked, and one of them was not a loop at all. **(1) The island sampler had no cancel at all.** `uniform_support_island()` and `uniform_support_peninsula()` (`SLA/SupportIslands/UniformSupportIsland.{hpp,cpp}`) now take a `ThrowOnCancel`, defaulted to the generator's named no-op, and it is asked inside `separate_thin_thick()` (per skeleton node), `get_line_indices()`, `merge_middle_parts_into_biggest_neighbor()`, `merge_same_neighbor_type_parts()`, `merge_short_parts()`, `get_longest_distance()`, `create_supports_for_thin_part()` / `_thick_part()`, `create_thick_field()`, `sample_outline()`, `sample_expolygon()` (per row of the pattern) and `sample_expolygons_with_centering()` (per region), and `align_samples()` asks between two iterations and `align_once()` every 256 samples and `move_duplicit_positions()` every pass. That is the step that never came back: the sampler walks a 1000 x 1000 mm region row by row on a ~1.4 mm grid, so a single layer is hundreds of thousands of samples and then a voronoi diagram over all of them. **(2) The generator asked per layer and nothing inside a layer.** `SupportPointGenerator.cpp` threads `throw_on_cancel` into `support_island()`, `support_peninsulas()`, `support_enforced_regions()`, `support_part_overhangs()`, `sample_overhangs()`, `filter_steep_overhangs()`, `prepare_supports_for_layer()`, `prepare_permanent_supports()`, `get_small_parts()` (per island inside the parallel loop), `is_reportable_island()` and `erase()`, and every one of them asks per sample, per point or per part instead of per layer. The parallel loops of `prepare_generator_data()` also stopped asking correctly: each of them only asked on `layer_id % 128` (or `% 16`, `% 32`), so a model with fewer layers than the step was asked on its first layer and never again - which is exactly the ten layers of this case. They ask on the first turn and then every n-th layer, and inside the layer per part as well. **(3) The tree builders asked per head but not per pillar and not inside a search.** `DefaultSupportTree` carries the `JobController::stopcondition` next to its `m_thr` and `interconnect_pillars()` asks per pillar of the cascade, per pillar of the extra-pillar pass and per round of the twenty tried angles. The searches are the reason this is its own item: NLopt reads `StopCriteria::stop_condition()` before every evaluation of the objective and calls `nlopt_force_stop()`, but nothing ever set it, so `get_criteria()` grew an overload that takes a `JobController::StopCond` and `optimize_pinhead_placement()`, `calculate_pinhead_placement()`, `deepsearch_ground_connection()` (all three overloads), `optimize_anchor_placement()`, `calculate_anchor_placement()`, `search_widening_path()` and `search_ground_route()` take one as a trailing parameter defaulting to `detail::never_stop`. Since M4.5c the searches of the different leaves run serialised under `nlopt_rng_lock()`, so one search is the whole run and nothing else could see the stop. The two brute-force walks below the searches (`deepsearch_ground_connection()`'s shortening loop and `connect_to_ground()`'s walk down) ask in their loop condition, so a run nobody stopped walks the very same steps. `BranchingTreeSLA.cpp` passes `builder.ctl().stopcondition` to the same functions and asks per head and per pillar of `build_pillars()`. **(4) The raft was built with the default cancel function.** `SupportTree.cpp` called `create_pad(sup_contours, model_contours, out, sm.pad_cfg)` without the `JobController`, so `create_pad_geometry()` asked a `pad_no_throw_on_cancel` that never throws - that is the "Could not create pad cavity" three minutes later in the log, on the far side of a stop that had already been asked for. It is handed `ctl.cancelfn` now, and `Pad.cpp` asks per pad part, per infill region, per hole, per piece of the raft floor, per connector-stick edge and per simplified slice of `pad_blueprint()`. `SupportFacetPaint.cpp` asks per layer of the painted regions. The two point transforms of `SLASupportTool.cpp` ask per 4096 points, so a run with millions of them can be given up between the generator and the tree. Nothing here changes a result unless a stop was asked for: every check either throws (the point generator and the pad, whose exceptions the tool turns into an empty result) or ends the loop that was already being given up (the tree), and `nlopt_force_stop()` only fires when the predicate says so.
    Tests: three cases in `tests/sla_print/sla_support_tool_tests.cpp`, all on the same 1000 mm cube the robustness case fails on, and all measuring the time from the moment the stop function first said yes to the moment the tool returned, which is what the todo asks for and not the whole run. The generator at a 100 mm layer height with the stop due after 150 ms; the default tree from 4000 island points on the bottom face with the stop due after 150 ms (four thousand pinheads, pillars and the raft cut out of the mesh of all of them); and a branching tree from 200 points with the stop due after 250 ms, which is the case that can only be given up from inside one of the serialised searches. Each asserts that the stop really came to be asked for, that the tool gave up within `stop_budget` (2 s) of it and that the answer is the empty one the API documents. NOT BUILT: a human build and run is needed before this can be called green.
    Left: the tool now stops, it is not fast - a metre wide cube still has to walk a metre wide layer of samples, and `NearPoints::add()` rebuilds the KD-tree of the whole layer on every point it is given, which is quadratic in the samples of a layer. That is a separate item (M4.6's speed half has no measurement yet) and nothing here depends on it.
  - [x] **M4.16-BuildFix11** M4.16 does not compile: two of the loops it opened in `SupportPointGenerator.cpp` were never closed, so every function below them was reported as a local function definition, and the point transform of `SLASupportTool.cpp` asks a `JobController` that is declared fifteen lines further down. · S · needs M4.16
    Result: `support_island()` of `SupportPointGenerator.cpp` had a `{` for its new `for (const SupportIslandPointPtr &sample : samples)` loop and no matching `}`, so the `}` after the `near_points.add(...)` closed the loop, nothing closed the function, and MSVC answered C2601 ("local function definitions are illegal") for every definition below it in the file: `filter_blocked_samples()` at 436, `support_enforced_regions()` at 461, `support_peninsulas()` at 536, `get_shapes()` at 570, `sample()` at 587, `sample_overhangs()` at 615, `filter_steep_overhangs()` at 635, `erase()` at 726 and the rest. That is one brace. `support_peninsulas()` was one short in the same way: M4.16 opened the brace of its inner `for (const SupportIslandPointPtr &support : peninsula_supports)` and never added the matching close, so the `}` after the `near_points.add(...)` closed the inner loop, the `}` after it closed the outer one and the function itself stayed open - which is also why the anonymous namespace that begins at the top of the file was never closed and the two brace counts of the file did not add up. Both braces are added; nothing else in either function moved. In `build_support_tree_for_tool()` of `SLASupportTool.cpp` the new walk over the support points asks `ctl.cancelfn()` every 4096 points, but the `sla::JobController ctl` that holds it is built after `SupportableMesh`, so the name was undeclared at the call (C2065). The controller is built before the walk now and not after it - it is built from `stop` alone and reads nothing of the mesh, so the two orderings are the same run, and the one that was there before M4.16 (no question asked on this walk at all) is a superset of it. Every other file of the M4.16 diff was then read against the three things this could have broken and needed nothing: the braces of all thirteen files balance (a scanner that skips comments, strings and raw strings), no added line is missing a semicolon, and every new `ThrowOnCancel` argument matches its declaration - `align_samples()` (the forward declaration at 246 carries the default, the definition at 557 does not), `get_longest_distance()`, `separate_thin_thick()`, `merge_middle_parts_into_biggest_neighbor()`, `merge_same_neighbor_type_parts()`, `merge_short_parts()`, `convert_island_parts_to_thin_thick()`, `sample_outline()`, `sample_expolygon()`, `sample_expolygons_with_centering()`, `create_thick_field()`, `get_line_indices()`, `align_once()`, `move_duplicit_positions()` and the two part samplers; the generator's nine functions and the four `execution::for_each` loops of `prepare_generator_data()` (which capture `throw_on_cancel` by value, as they did for the loop bodies that were already there); `get_criteria()`'s new overload and the seven search and walk functions that take a `JobController::StopCond` (`optimize_pinhead_placement`, `calculate_pinhead_placement`, the three `deepsearch_ground_connection` overloads, `optimize_anchor_placement`, `calculate_anchor_placement`, `search_widening_path`, `search_ground_route`, `connect_to_ground`), which `BranchingTreeSLA.cpp` and `DefaultSupportTree.cpp` call with `ctl().stopcondition` and `m_stopcond`; the `breakstick_holes` pack of `Pad.cpp`, whose `thr` sits before `Args...` in the declaration and after it in the expansion, which is the order its calls give; `cut_infill_cells()`, `create_infill_geometry()`, `create_infill_floor()`, `create_pad_geometry()`, `create_pad()`; the two loops of `SupportFacetPaint.cpp`; and `create_pad(sup_contours, model_contours, out, sm.pad_cfg, ctl.cancelfn)` in `SupportTree.cpp`. The two lambdas of the diff that read `thr` read it out of a parameter that declares it (the `get_small_parts` island loop captures it by value, the `remove_redundant_parts` predicate of `Pad.cpp` likewise), and the three new cases of `sla_support_tool_tests.cpp` put no `&&`, `||` or ternary inside a `CHECK` or a `REQUIRE`. No behaviour change: a run nobody stopped walks the same steps and produces the same points. NOT BUILT (a human builds and runs the tests in this session); NOT CHECKED in a running app.

Two things M5.1's own text does not ask for are still missing: the archive's print settings are read but not applied to the presets, and there is no progress bar, because the shared import path is synchronous for every format.
  Split 2026-09-22: upstream removed SLA import outright in `d9e89cf564` ("Remove SLA import", 901 lines over 11 files), so there is no reader to wire up; it has to be restored first.
  - [x] **M5.1a** Restore the SL1/SL1S archive reader into libslic3r from `d9e89cf564^` (`SLAArchiveReader`, `ZipperArchiveImport`, the reading half of `SL1`), plus the deleted round-trip test. Engine only, no UI. Do not restore the engine-level `SLAArchiveFormatRegistry`: format registration now lives in `Biz/ResultExport/SLA/SlaArchiveFormat` (M0.9). · M · needs M0.10
    Result (2026-09-29): restored as the split the Blocked note asked for. M5.1a1: `sla::layers_to_mesh` in the public `libslic3r/SLALayersToMesh.hpp`. M5.1a2: `import_sl1_archive` in `Biz/ResultExport/SLA/SL1Import` opens the zip with MiniZWrapper, parses `config.ini`/`prusaslicer.ini` with a plain key=value reader (no `DynamicPrintConfig`), decodes the layer pngs with `png::decode_png` and calls the engine function; a round-trip test slices a 20 mm cube, exports it with `store_sl1` and reads it back, plus the error cases. Wiring into the UI is M5.1b. Both halves are ticked below.
  - [x] **M5.1a1** Engine: one public function that turns decoded layer images into a mesh. · M · needs M0.10
    Result: `sla::layers_to_mesh()` in `libslic3r/SLALayersToMesh.hpp` takes decoded grayscale layers plus pixel size, display size, mirroring and layer heights and returns a `Domain::TriangleMesh`: marching squares per layer, the raster trafo inverted so a rendered layer comes back in place, then the outlines stacked with `slices_to_mesh()`. Only public Domain headers are used in the header, so Biz can import archives without reaching libslic3r's private ones. Tested: both suites green 2026-09-30.
  - [x] **M5.1a2** Biz: open the archive, parse its ini files and decode its layer images. · M · needs M5.1a1
    Result: `import_sl1_archive()` in `Biz/ResultExport/SLA/SL1Import` opens a `.sl1`/`.sl1s` with `MiniZWrapper`, reads `config.ini` and `prusaslicer.ini` with a plain key=value parser, sorts the layer images by name for the layer order and decodes each to 8-bit gray with `png::decode_png` (gray first, rgba collapsed to luminance as a fallback). Display size, pixel counts, orientation, mirroring and layer heights come from the profile keys, with `config.ini`'s `layerHeight` as the layer-height fallback; missing metadata, an archive without layer images and an undecodable layer are errors. A round-trip test slices a 20 mm cube off both display centre lines, exports it with `store_sl1` and reads it back, plus hand-built error cases. Tested: both suites green 2026-09-30.

    Result (2026-09-29): `.sl1` and `.sl1s` are in `get_import_extensions()`, so the File > Import dialog, the "All supported files" pattern and the drag and drop filter all take them; `read_data_from_file()` calls `import_sl1_archive` and hands the mesh to the shared path, which adds it as a model object named after the file, and the reader's message goes out through the usual "Files import" error dialog. The archive's print settings are read but not applied to the presets (later step). No job: the shared import path (`ProjectInteractor::load_models_to_project` from the File menu and from the drop target) is synchronous for every format, so there is no progress bar or stop flag to pass. The millimetre scale of the reconstructed mesh comes from the profile, so the meters/inches guessing of the mesh formats is skipped for archives. No test: `FileLoadingLogic` has no test file, so the extension list is not covered.
  - [x] **M5.1c** The archive's print settings are offered after the import, and the archive import itself runs as a job with progress and cancel. · M · needs M5.1b
    Result: this is the work M5.1's own result note lists as still missing (the settings are read but not applied, and there is no progress bar); the note above is left as it was. **Settings.** `SlaArchiveSettingsNotification` (App/Plater) listens to `on_instance_added`, finds the archive the new object came from in its `ModelVolume::Source::input_file`, and runs the existing resin import as a **dry run** through `ResinProfileImportInteractor`. If the mapping writes at least one resin value it shows a notification with one button, "Use the settings from this archive", which opens the M3.10 review on that archive; the user reads what each value becomes there and saves them as a user resin preset. Nothing is written and the selected printer is never touched, so the offer is the only thing that happens unasked. Two gaps had to be closed for the review to be usable at all: `default_registry()` registered only `ChituboxCfgReader`, so an `.sl1` was not readable by the dialog or by `--import-resin-profile` at all (it now calls `register_resin_profile_readers()`), and the mapping table had none of the archive's own spellings — `expTime`, `expTimeFirst` and `numFade` are now sources next to the Chitubox ones (`layerHeight` was already there). All three are same-unit copies, `store_sl1()` writes them straight off the config keys. The archive's bookkeeping (`jobDir`, `action`, `printTime`, `usedMaterial`, `numSlow`, `numFast`, `hollow`, ...) is now one reported rule instead of a dozen Unknown rows. The supports, raft and hollowing of the archive's prusaslicer.ini have no resin mapping, so `App/Plater/SlaArchiveSettings` names them in the notification ("Not applied, these are print settings: ...", config keys, capped at 6 with the rest counted) rather than dropping them in silence. **Progress and cancel.** `read_sla_archives()` in `Biz/FileLoadingLogic` is the job body with the threads taken out (`import_sla_archives_local()` is the JobManager adapter over it); `import_sl1_archive()` and `sla::layers_to_mesh()` grew an optional progress callback, decoding the layer images being two thirds of the bar and tracing them into a mesh the rest. `ProjectInteractor::load_models_to_project()` splits the archives off and starts the job `sla_archive_import`; every other format keeps the synchronous path it had. The mesh goes on the plate, is arranged and gets its undo step in `on_result`, back on the main thread, and a cancelled job returns nothing so nothing reaches the project. `JobNotificationSpec` grew a `cancelable` flag and a new `PopNotificationLayoutHeaderProgressButtons` layout puts a Cancel button on the running job's bar, which calls `request_job_stop`; the existing arrange, simplify and repair notifications are unchanged. **Tests.** `SlaArchiveImportJobTests.cpp` drives the job body with a fake reader: the progress is monotonic from 0 to 1, a cancel asked for before, during or between archives adds nothing, a broken archive among good ones is reported without stopping the rest, and `is_sla_archive_file()` decides what counts. `SlaArchiveSettingsTests.cpp` checks that the archive's spellings reach the resin keys, that the notification is built with the action for an archive with settings and is not built at all for one without, that the button opens the review, and that the not-applied names and their cap are right. Not built or run in this session; the human builds and runs the suites. Fix 2026-10-01 (found by a test build): the case asked for `material_density` among the applied values, but the label table of `analyze_archive_settings()` calls that value "Density", so the list holds labels and not resin keys; the value of that archive the table has no label for is the bottle size, so the case asks for `bottle_volume`. No product change.

Method written up in `doc/sla-fork/formats/orientation.md`, without numbers.   Progress 2026-09-30: the removable-drive half is done and tested. The destination already went through the shared path (`ExportActions::make_export` → `show_export_modal_dialog` → `ProjectInteractor::do_result_export`), so the extension came from `SlaExportFileTypes`, the M5.9 unfinished-slice check and the error reporting applied, and `PrintHostLocal` reported the target, which is what puts Eject on the finished-export notification; there was no FFF-only or SLA-only gate in any of it. The one thing that blocked it: `PhysicalPrinterInteractor::is_printer_compatible` accepted only the hard-coded default entry (Local Drive) for a filesystem destination, so Removable Drive came back "incompatible" and `on_selected_config_container_changed()` reset the destination to the local one as soon as the printer or the plate changed, putting the file on the local disk instead of the stick. A filesystem destination never talks to a printer, so it is now compatible with every profile, FFF and SLA alike. Left: nothing uploads to a print host yet, and no test covers the destination logic (there is no fixture for `PhysicalPrinterInteractor`). Both suites green 2026-09-30.

  - [x] **M5.5b** Upload the exported SLA archive to a print host. Survey first which of the print hosts can take a resin file at all: the SL1 runs PrusaLink's firmware and speaks its REST API, so PrusaLink and Prusa Connect take `.sl1`/`.sl1s`, and the rest (OctoPrint, Moonraker, Duet, FlashAir, AstroBox, Repetier, MKS) are FFF servers that take G-Code. A host that cannot read the printer's format has to say so rather than upload. · M · needs —
    Result: the rule is one pure Biz function, `PrintHostFormats` (`print_host_accepts_extension`, `destination_accepts_extension`, `destination_rejects_extension_message`), with 5 Catch2 cases over every host type, a filesystem destination and Prusa Connect; no network. The upload path needed no new plumbing: `ExportActions::upload_gcode_to_print_host` -> `show_upload_modal_dialog` -> `do_result_upload` already ended in the same `do_result_export_inner` as the file export, so the extension came from `SlaExportFileTypes`, the M5.9 unfinished-slice check and the M5.9a failure notification applied, and the finalizer writes the archive with the format registry. Two things were missing. The upload dialog took a typed name without asking whether it fits the plate's format, so it now says what the export dialog says and offers to choose again (`warn_sla_format_mismatch`), and its title is "Send print file to printer host" on an SLA bed instead of "Send G-Code to printer host". ## M6: Quality gates and release

- [x] **M6.1** Robustness mesh set with no crashes or hangs (PLAN G2). · M · needs M0.13
  Progress: `tests/sla_print/sla_robustness_tests.cpp` builds 11 bad meshes in code (empty, single triangle, degenerate triangle, open box, box with a flipped face, two overlapping cubes in one volume, cube with duplicated faces, 0.05 mm cube, 0.02 mm plate, 0.5 × 0.5 × 150 mm needle, cube 1500 mm from the origin) and runs each through support point generation, the support tree and raft, and a full slice. Every support call gets a 60 s stop function; the far-from-origin case runs both stages on a worker thread with a deadline, because the support generator has run for minutes on a mesh at ~1364 mm. Not built: the test target was not compiled, so nothing is known about what the pipeline does with these meshes yet. Left unticked until it passes and until its `needs` (M0.13) is done.
    Progress 2026-09-30: the test is compiled and runs in `sla_print_tests` (108/108 pass), so the 11 bad meshes go through support point generation, the support tree and raft and a full slice without a crash or a hang, and the 60 s stop function and the worker-thread deadline on the far-from-origin case do their job. Build 32 also hardened the two reads this set ran into: `SLAPrint::Steps::support_points` no longer reads `m_model_height_levels[1]` when the object is one layer tall or thinner (the assert that covered it is compiled out in Release; it falls back to `Domain::sla_effective_layer_height()` with a warning), `initialize_printer_input()` checks the slice index before taking `m_slice_index.front()` and no longer assigns a bool to the layer count, and `apply_printer_corrections()` tests the `m_supportable_mesh` optional. Still unticked: the todo's `needs` (M0.13, the benchmark harness) is not done, so a fixture-only pass over synthetic meshes is not yet the robustness run the todo asks for.
  Result: the set is generated in code (no files from tests/data) and now holds 20 bad meshes: empty, single triangle, zero area triangle, open box, box with a square hole in a face, box with one flipped face, box with every normal inverted, box with three faces on one edge (a non-manifold edge), cube with its faces twice, box with one triangle twice at the same coordinates, two overlapping cubes in one volume, two crossing shells, two cubes touching at a face, 0.05 mm cube, 0.02 mm plate, 0.1 x 0.1 x 150 mm needle, a one metre cube, a cube 100 km from the origin, a cube floating 50 mm above the plate with supports off, and a box with a NaN and an infinity among its vertices. Every case runs the support tool (points, then tree and raft) and a full SLAPrint slice on a worker thread under a watchdog: `std::async` plus `wait_for`, and a stage that overruns its budget is asked to stop (the support tool through its stop function, the slice through `IPrint::stop_token`, the same handle BackgroundProcess uses) and gets 30 s to unwind; a stage that neither finishes nor takes the stop fails the test instead of hanging the binary. Only a `Slic3r::RuntimeError` or a `Slic3r::Biz::Slicing::Exception` carrying a message counts as a handled refusal, anything else escaping a stage is recorded and fails the case. Two guards went in with it: `SLAPrint::slice()` refuses a model whose mesh has a vertex that is not a number before the first step reads it (the bounding box helpers assert on such a box and `scaled()` of it is not a level, so the slice index would run across the whole coordinate range), and `compute_slice_heights()` in the support tool returns no heights for a box that is not a range of coordinates or a layer height that is not a step, which is the loop that never ends on them. Not built and not run here (a human builds and runs both suites); nothing was checked in the app. M0.13 stays open and is the separate benchmark run this set feeds — the set itself does not need it.

The same rule is a unit test in `FixtureRenderTests.cpp`, so a palette change cannot collapse the model, the supports and the pad unnoticed; the python tool is run over fixtures made for it. `doc/sla-fork/visual-regression.md` has the commands and how to make a reference. `visual_diff.py` and its lightness math were run here; the app was not built, so the render path and the new tests are not.

- [x] **M6.3** Nightly upstream merge rehearsal with a conflict report (PLAN G4). · S · needs M0.3, M0.14
  Result: `doc/sla-fork/tools/upstream_rehearsal.py` (standard library only) is the whole rehearsal, and it is local first. It fetches the read-only `upstream` remote, counts the upstream commits since the last merge base, then creates a throwaway worktree under the gitignored `.agent-scratch/`, detached at the branch tip, runs `git merge --no-commit --no-ff` there, counts the conflict hunks of every unmerged file and writes a Markdown report (and `--json` for CI), and aborts the merge and removes the worktree in a `finally`. No branch can move, because a merge in a detached worktree cannot update one; nothing is ever pushed; the tip is read again at the end and the report says whether it moved. Conflicted files are grouped as engine SLA / config defs / App/Biz / build / other, each with its hunk count and whether it is a PLAN 3.3 hotspot file, and the hotspots get their own section, because those are merged ahead of other work in small PRs. Exit code 0 clean, 1 conflicts, 2 the run itself failed. `.github/workflows/upstream_rehearsal.yml` is `workflow_dispatch` only and is its own small file, since M0.14 has no workflow file yet: a full clone (a shallow one has no merge base), the upstream remote added by the job, the report in the job summary and uploaded as an artifact, and a conflict fails the job only when the input says so. The commands, the remotes and the exit codes are in [ci.md](ci.md). `doc/sla-fork/tools/test_upstream_rehearsal.py` tests the report builder as a pure function on a made up conflict list (22 unittest cases: area grouping, the hotspot list, hunk counts, the Markdown, the JSON, the exit code). Not run yet: no merge was rehearsed here, so the first real run, on a machine with the upstream remote, is a person's job.
  Left: PLAN G4 also asks for a build and a test run of the merged tree. That is not in the script (it would need the whole build, which is what the M0.14 runner is for) and is not done; the workflow is manual rather than nightly, so the schedule line in ci.md is the follow-up once M0.14 lands; and the todo's `needs` M0.14 is still open.

    Result: docs only, no workflow and no code changed, and nothing was run. `doc/sla-fork/ci.md` is now one document with one H1: an overview (both workflows are manual only, no `push` and no `schedule`, neither has ever been executed on a runner), `sla-ci.yml` (what a run does, the by-hand and `run-ci` label triggers, the cost, the timeouts, the `layer_hash` gate and the six steps that were never verified), the upstream rehearsal (local use of `upstream_rehearsal.py`, what one run does, the report, the exit codes, the report builder tests and the manual workflow), and a short note that the upstream workflows in `.github/workflows` (`build_windows`, `build_osx`, `build_flatpak` and `clang_format` on every push, `static_analysis` nightly) stay untouched for mergeability, so Actions on this fork should be disabled or restricted to the two workflows. - [x] **M6.2d** Nothing runs the tool in CI yet: add a job beside M0.14's that renders the fixtures, runs `visual_diff.py` against the committed references and fails on a changed image or on a palette lightness the PLAN 2.1 rule rejects. · M · needs M6.2, M0.14
  Result: a `visual-regression` job in `.github/workflows/sla-ci.yml` beside `build-and-test`, with the same manual gate (workflow_dispatch, or the `run-ci` label on a pull request) and its own runner, sharing the deps cache key. It starts cheap: `test_visual_ci.py`, then `doc/sla-fork/tools/visual_ci.py plan`, which reads `doc/sla-fork/visual-regression/manifest.json` (id, fixture, view, size, reference, probes - the layout is in visual-regression.md, written by whoever makes the first references) and writes `have_references`; every step that costs minutes is skipped while that is false, so with no committed references the job is green and says why instead of building an app to compare against nothing. Otherwise it builds `slic3r-app-launcher` (the binary that takes `--render-to`), renders every entry under `xvfb-run` with llvmpipe - the render is offscreen but the GL context belongs to the window - and runs `visual_diff.py check` on it, which fails the job on a changed image or on a lightness pair the PLAN 2.1 rule rejects. `visual_ci.py` is the glue, standard library only: the manifest, the two command lines, the loop, the Markdown report and the exit codes (0 passed or nothing to compare, 1 a render changed, 2 the run could not be made) are pure functions, so `test_visual_ci.py` covers them in 48 unit tests with a fake app and a fake `visual_diff.py` and needs no build; the loop was also run end to end here against a fake app writing real PNGs and a real sidecar (a pass, a changed picture and a 2 on a refused render). Neither the tool nor the job has been run against a real app or on a runner, and the render path itself is still unbuilt (M6.2a, M6.2b and M6.2c are open). `ci.md` and visual-regression.md carry the cost, the exit codes, the manifest layout and the first-run watch list: headless GL, a dialog that could block a render, and whether a Linux render matches a Windows reference at all.
- [x] **M6.2b** `--render-view preview` draws the sliced print the way the objects list thumbnails do, not the Preview tab with its own camera, so a change in that view is invisible to the check: render the Preview view itself. · M · needs M6.2

    scene, its own `PreviewSceneRenderCustomizer` and its camera as the `CameraSynchData` the two views hand
    each other on a tab switch (`current_camera_synch_data()`, which `on_deactivated` uses now too). The app
    hands the source to `ThumbnailImageGenerator::set_fixture_view_source`, the render goes through
    `generate_thumbnails` with the view's customizer and a camera built by `set_fixture_view_camera()` - the
    same `synchronize_camera` the modules use, so the eye, the target, the angles and the zoom of the view are
    what a change in the view has to show up in, and the viewport of the size asked for is the only thing a
    render changes. The gcode-thumbnail path is out of the render: a preview render draws the Preview scene and
    not the plater one. The sidecar grew a `scene` key ("plater" / "preview") and a view with no print in its
    scene is not rendered: `fixture_render_has_print()` skips the bed, and `do_fixture_render` asks a view again
    instead of writing a reference of an empty bed. 91 lines of tests in `FixtureRenderTests.cpp`: the view to
    scene mapping, the camera handover (zoom, projection, the eye at the distance of the target, the forward
    axis, a second view giving a second camera, an orthographic view rendered orthographic, the viewport left
    to the renderer) and the sidecar key. The render path itself is GL and has no unit test; nothing was built
    or run here, and M6.2a is still the first end to end run. The M6.2 note above still says the preview draws
    the objects list thumbnail: that line is left as it was written.

    `doc/sla-fork/visual-regression.md` in a new **What a render is not** section: a table of what is in a render and

    markers, the legend, pop-ups and dialogs; the cursor and the hover state a mouse gives the UI), why it is a
    separate job and not a flag (the UI is ImGui drawn by `render_imgui` into the canvas, a render draws the scene
    into a framebuffer of its own, so a window render needs a fixed window size, a framebuffer of the window to
    read back - a `Render::Framebuffer` is always one the device created - an ImGui pass with a known state, and a
    forced theme and scale factor, which alone decides it: a window reference would differ between a light and a
    dark desktop and between two scale factors), and what would have to happen first, in order. The list keeps it.
    The record is in the artefact too, not only in the text: `FixtureRenderCoverage` and `fixture_render_coverage()`
    (App/FixtureRender.hpp) put a `covers` object into every sidecar - `view: true`, `window`, `overlays` and
    `cursor: false` today - so a reference says what it is a reference of and a diff of two sidecars points at a
    reference that stopped covering the same thing. 24 lines of tests in `FixtureRenderTests.cpp` pin the coverage
    of both views and the four sidecar keys. Not built and not run here, and nothing was rendered; the window render
    is not started, it is the list.

## M7: Excellent auto-supports

> **Parked (2026-09-18).** The priority is the regular support function: the support points tool (M2.1–M2.3) and support generation quality (M4.3–M4.5). M7.4 work in progress is on the unmerged branch `sla/M7.4-rule-mining`. Known issues there: tip width saturates at the search radius, and registration error (0.5–0.9 mm RMS) exceeds the contact gap, so most contacts are missed. Refine registration on the model-only vertices before detecting contacts.

**Goal:** on typical models (miniatures, busts, functional parts), auto-generated supports print reliably and match what an experienced person would do. Someone experienced shouldn't *need* to hand-edit them, and cleanup should leave no scars on detailed or cosmetic surfaces.

**Approach**
1. **Interview:** write down expert knowledge as explicit rules (M7.1).
2. **Research dataset:** supported scenes made by an experienced person in another slicer (currently Lychee), exported as plain meshes by that slicer (M7.2).
3. **Offline analysis:** scripts in `tools/support-research/` (Python, not part of the app build) extract every support contact (M7.3) and mine measurable rules from them (M7.4).
4. **Rulebook:** interview rules and mined rules are merged and reviewed (M7.5).
5. **Scoring:** the generator is scored against the expert supports, with a baseline first (M7.6, M7.7).
6. **Implementation:** the generator is improved rule by rule, with every change scored, then tuned (M7.8–M7.11).
7. **Confirmation:** real prints check the result (M7.12).

**Research-only rules**
- **Nothing is ported.** The dataset is only for *learning rules*. PrusaSLA gets no reader for other slicers' project formats, and the data comes from the authoring slicer's own mesh export. Don't decode `.lys` or other protected project files.
- **Personal-use license.** The current dataset is Factory Fortress Trench Crusade miniatures. Their Terms of Use grant a personal-use, single-computer license; forbid sharing, distribution and commercial or employer use; and allow modification only for personal use. So:
  - **Files stay in place.** Leave them in the owner's own folder, referenced through a manifest. Don't copy them into the repo tree or into `local-samples/`.
  - **Files never leave this computer.** No CI, cloud agents, uploads or file sharing.
  - **Agents don't open mesh files** in their context. They run local analysis scripts and read only the aggregate output.
  - **Only aggregate numbers are committed**, such as spacing, angles and diameters, never geometry or per-model contact lists. The rulebook, and synthetic test shapes made from scratch, can be committed.
  - **It stays a personal research activity.** If this work is done for an employer, the owner must check the license first.

- [ ] **M7.3** Contact extraction script. · L → split · needs M7.2
  - **Register the pair.** A check on 3 pairs (2026-09-17) found the supported STLs are **not** in the unsupported model's frame: they are rotated and moved into print orientation, and sometimes remeshed (one head had 31k triangles supported vs 260k unsupported). No triangles matched even at 0.01 mm. So:
    - Estimate the rigid transform (rotation + translation, no scale) from the unsupported model into the supported file: coarse alignment (principal axes plus the ambiguous flips, or feature-based global registration), then point-to-plane ICP on the model surface only, ignoring the support region as outliers.
    - Report the residual (RMS and 95th-percentile surface distance) and the recovered print tilt. Reject pairs above tolerance.
  - **Separate supports from the model:** after registration, take faces of the supported mesh farther than ε from the transformed model surface. Triangle-exact matching must not be assumed, because the model may be remeshed.
  - **Record orientation:** the recovered rotation is itself data for the orientation rules (M7.4).
  - **Describe each contact:** position, surface normal, contact diameter, penetration estimate, height above the plate, local overhang angle and curvature, and whether it serves a new island or local minimum (found by slicing the model mesh into layers).
  - **Record structure:** base/raft type and trunk and branch counts.
  - **Outputs** (under `local-samples/supports/out/`, gitignored and never committed): `contacts.json`, plus a colored debug mesh for a quick check by eye.

  Done when: a synthetic test shape with procedurally placed supports, then rotated, moved and remeshed, gives back the known transform and every known contact within tolerance.

  Result: `separate_supports_scaled()` in `tools/support-research/separate.py`. Nearest-sample distance bounds the true distance from above, so a face is model as soon as that bound is below epsilon (no margin), while the support side needs a safety margin derived from the achieved sample spacing; only the band between is measured exactly. Sample count follows surface area with a cap, and scene faces are processed in chunks. Synthetic cone tips (radius 0.6 mm, height 1.2 mm) yield measured diameter 1.2 mm (2×TIP_RADIUS), vertex_count ≥ 17 (all tip vertices in width_band), penetration ~0, overhang ~0°, serves_local_minimum=True. Contacts.json and debug mesh outputs verified.
  - [x] **M7.3e** Use the shell structure of the supported files instead of registering against the whole scene: a slicer writes the model, every support, tree and raft as its own body, so welding the vertices and labelling the faces by adjacency splits a supported STL into the model shell and the support shells, with no registration, no epsilon and no resampling. Register the plain model against the model shell alone (the largest shell of the scene) and count the contacts on the support shells where they stand, since both are in one file and one frame. Done when: on a synthetic scene the split finds the model and every support, the tip counts and diameter classes come back exactly, and registering the plain model against its own shell recovers a known rotation. · M · needs M7.3a, M7.3b2, M7.3c
  Result: `split_supported_scene()` in the new `tools/support-research/shells.py` welds a supported file at 1 um (`calibrate.weld`, far below the mesh resolution and far above the rounding of a written STL), labels the faces by adjacency with `calibrate.connected_components` (networkx is not installed, and a numpy union-find is enough), and takes the shell with the most faces as the model: on the five head pairs the supervisor measured that shell at 77-99% of the scene's faces, with the face count and the extents of the plain model up to rotation. The other shells come back as support shells, each with only the faces and the vertices it uses. `calibrate.py measure_pair` registers the plain model against the model shell alone, which is model against model, and the contacts come off the support shells in place: a tip is a cluster of the shell's own vertices within 0.5 mm of the model shell, clustered per shell at 0.5 mm, and its diameter estimate is the largest extent of the cluster; per shell it also records the height above the plate (the lowest z of the scene is the plate), whether it reaches the plate, and whether it is a raft (flat, wide and on the plate), aggregated per pair into the tip count, the tip count per diameter class (0.1/0.2/0.3/0.4/0.6 mm, nearest), the structure count and the structures on the plate. The record schema is kept (`calibration.json` is now schema 2 and adds `scene_shells`, `scene_model_faces`, `scene_model_face_fraction`, `shell_tips`, `shell_tips_by_diameter_mm`, `shell_tip_diameter_median_mm`, `shell_structures`, `shell_structures_on_plate`, `shell_rafts`, `shell_height_max_mm`, `supports_separate`; `calibration_report.py` reads the old keys and needs no change), the summary line gained `split=` and `tip_classes=`, and `--epsilon` is gone with the distance method, which `count_support_tips` keeps for its own tests. The registrations the dataset needs are back inside the 0.9 inlier criterion at 0.3 mm that `register()` already enforces: the criterion was not lowered. 23 tests pass in the new `test_m73e.py` (synthetic scenes from `synth.py`: a model plus N cone supports with known tip sizes, split finds the model and N supports, the tip diameters and classes come back exactly, a raft is a base and not a tip, a tree that stops short of the model is no tip, two heads within one cluster radius are still two tips, and the registration of the model against its own shell recovers the known rotation), and the whole support-research suite is 148 tests in ~95 s. NOT RUN on real data: no mesh of the dataset was opened and no pair was measured here, so the split, the registration and the contacts are verified on synthetic scenes and on one synthetic pair driven through the real script (STL in, exit 0, inlier 0.999, five tips in five classes, the oriented STL written). Unverified on the dataset: that the largest shell is the model on every pair of it (one pair has its model shell decimated to 0.09 of the plain model's faces, so the face-count rule has to be read per pair), what a Lychee tip measures as against its own preset size, whether any pair's bodies touch and would then weld into one shell (`supports_separate` says so in the record), and the time and memory of the split and the one distance query on a 1-2 million face scene.

  Result: `tools/support-research/calibrate.py` registers every pair of the manifest (unsupported -> supported, model surface only, M7.3a), separates the support faces (M7.3b2) and records per pair into the gitignored `local-samples/supports/out/calibration.json`: id, category, model size in the expert print orientation, the registration residual (RMS, p95, inlier fraction), the tilt of the modelling-up axis (+Z of the plain STL) and the direction it points in, whether the model's largest flat area faces the plate, and the expert's support tips, structures, structures that reach the plate or a raft, and bases. A tip is a cluster of support vertices within 1.5 mm of the transformed model surface, clustered per connected component, so a branching tree counts its tips instead of one, and a raft does not count as a tip at all (its top face is under every model that sits on it); the same generous gap is what makes the count survive the known 0.5-0.9 mm registration error, at the price of never being a contact position. It also writes the plain model rotated into the expert orientation as `out/oriented/<id>.stl` (`--clean` removes them), and exits 0 / 1 / 2 with a summary line of counts only: no path and no per-pair number reaches the console. `tests/sla_print/sla_calibration_support_tests.cpp` (new, hidden `[.local]`, skipped unless `SLA_CALIB_MANIFEST` is set, one CMake line) runs `sla::generate_support_points_for_tool` with the default SLA config over each plain model as loaded and centred on the plate, and over the oriented copy when calibrate.py wrote one, and prints one `[CalibSupport] id=..., category=..., points_as_loaded=..., points_expert_orientation=..., islands=..., height_mm=..., height_expert_mm=...` line per pair, ids and categories only, with the loader messages scrubbed of every path. `tools/support-research/calibration_report.py` merges that log with the JSON and writes Markdown tables by category (support density with the expert/ours ratio in both orientations, orientation with the up-axis tilt and the shares face-up and 30-60 deg, structure and registration residual), never an id: the supervisor commits that table to `doc/sla-fork/supports/calibration.md`. The measurement functions of both scripts (manifest reader, weld, connected components, tip counting, up-axis tilt, largest flat area, the tables) import numpy alone, so `test_m74c.py` covers them on a box, a bust, pillars and point rings written from scratch (synth.py needs trimesh, which is the dependency these tests are there to avoid): 50 tests pass in 0.7 s with numpy and pytest only, no trimesh, no scipy, no mesh and no dataset. NOT BUILT and NOT RUN: the C++ test was never compiled, neither script was ever run against the real dataset (scipy and trimesh are not installed here and nothing was downloaded), so no table exists yet. A class is more than its tip: `SlaSupportPreset` carries a `SlaSupportGeometry` next to the three sizes that are settings, so a preset button lands all of R3 at once - a ball contact (R3.1), the cone under it (R3.2), a hexagonal stem of 1.0 mm (R3.3) and the one 6 mm by 0.3 mm prism foot (R3.4) - on the next clicked point and on a selection in one undo step, knot included. The depth of the contact is derived and not stored: `sla_support_contact_depth(tip)` is half the tip (0.05 / 0.1 / 0.15 / 0.2 / 0.3 mm), which a preset writes on the point and `SlaSupportPointsEditing::add_point` stamps through the new `contact_depth_mm`, so a class whose tip a print preset overrides sinks half of *that* tip. One number of R3.2 is not expressible in the mesher and is left: the neck is to widen to 0.8 mm, while `sla::pinhead()` builds a single tangent cone from the contact to the stem whose back radius *is* the stem radius (`sla::head_back_radius()`, `SupportTreeUtils.hpp:331`), so with the R3.3 stem of 1.0 mm it widens to 1.0 mm. What the presets do instead is the half of R3.2 the tree can give: the cone is 0.5 mm long, so the break point sits right under the ball, and it starts at the contact radius as it always has rather than at the 0.8-0.95 x tip the rulebook names. What changed for it: the base of the model takes preset button 3, which is the `heavy` id and, since M7.8.1, the T0.4 mm class, and the three choices of `support_auto_detail_preset` read as tip sizes (0.1, 0.2, 0.3) like the preset rows above them while their stored ids stay `mini`, `light` and `medium`, so a project or print preset that carries the key keeps meaning the same class.     Result: by OpenCode, NOT BUILT (a human builds and runs the tests). Every generated point now carries `SLA::SupportPoint::Role` (Anchor / Island / SmallIsland / Overhang / Fragile, compared in `operator==` and written out with the other per-point fields, as `role` in the 3MF and in the undo snapshots, Unknown for a project of before). The new `sla::classify_support_point_roles()` in `SLA/SupportRoles.{hpp,cpp}` runs from `generate_support_points_for_tool` right after `move_on_mesh_surface`: the island points within two layers of the lowest point of the object are anchors (R4.1), the other island points are islands or small islands by the area of the layer part they are on, 1 mm2 being the line (R4.3), a point on a feature under 1.2 mm - measured by a ray into the model along the inverted normal, by the footprint of its layer part, or by the width of the part over the first layers above a spike tip - is fragile (R4.4), and everything else is an overhang (R4.6). R4.5 comes first and is what can move a point: a point whose surface stands out of the best-fit plane of the surface 2.6 mm around it - more than 0.15 mm out of it, and no further out than the distance a point may move, which is what a stud is and a gap under a brim is not; the two rings inside the far one are what tell a bump from a curve - moves to the nearest plain spot within 1 mm that faces down at least as steeply and is still on the island or the overhang it carried, and becomes fragile when there is no such spot. The head radius of a point is the one the generator gave it, and which points are generated does not change otherwise. `rulebook_tip_class(Role)` in App/Plater is the pure mapping of a role to a preset name that the auto support classifier of M2.37 asks for (Anchor heavy, Island medium, SmallIsland and Overhang and Unknown light, Fragile mini); the two completion handlers and `support_geometry_defaults()` are untouched, so the role reaches the tool without the tool writing sizes itself.   - [x] **M7.8.3** Heavy anchors (R4.2): a few T0.4 anchors on flat, low-detail areas facing the plate, scaled with the model's size; T0.6 on very large objects. · M · needs M7.8.1
    Result: by OpenCode, NOT BUILT (a human builds and runs the tests). The new `sla::add_heavy_anchors()` in `SLA/SupportAnchors.{hpp,cpp}` runs from `generate_support_points_for_tool` right after `classify_support_point_roles`, and puts the heavy anchors of R4.2 on a model: a few points of the `Anchor` role on the flat, low-detail areas of the surface that faces the build plate. The candidate spots are the centres of a 3 mm grid over the facets that face the plate (normal z <= -0.5) in the lower part of the model (the lowest quarter of its height, and never less than 6 mm), and a spot may carry an anchor when the surface around it is flat over a ~3 mm neighbourhood and the feature under it is not thin: a ray into the model measures the thickness the way M7.8.2 measures it for R4.4, and sixteen rays are cast from under the eight spots around the spot at two distances, every one of which has to come out in the plane the spot is on, which is the measure M7.8.2 moves a point off a stud with for R4.5. Both ask the surface and not the facets, so a stud a millimetre across is found whatever the mesh is cut into and a finely tessellated flat underside is not mistaken for detail. Nothing to measure in a direction is a spot at the rim of a model, on the cap of a bump or under the roof of a cavity, and none of those may carry an anchor. The spots are ranked by the flat surface around them, by how low they are on the model and by how far they are from the anchors it already has, and the best N of them take one: a point of the generator that already stands on a spot becomes that anchor (an overhang or an island point, never a fragile one and never a hand-placed one, and never a second anchor beside it), and a spot with nothing on it gets a new island point at least 3 mm from every other point of the model. N is 2 for a footprint that fits in 30 x 30 mm and grows by one per 400 mm2 above that, never more than 8, and the grid of a very large footprint is widened so that the pass looks for at most 4096 spots, which is what keeps a metre-wide plate inside its budget. A footprint above 150 x 150 mm is a very large object, and every anchor of it takes the largest tip (T0.6): the new `SupportPoint::Role::AnchorLarge`, appended at the end of the enum with nothing renumbered, which `rulebook_tip_class` in the tool answers with the "xheavy" preset. The anchors M7.8.2 already made of the lowest island are the same class on such a model. Tests: seven cases in `tests/sla_print/sla_support_anchor_tests.cpp` - the miniature with studs hanging under it gets two anchors on the flat underside, none on a bump and none on the fragile point of R4.4; a 40 x 40 and a 60 x 60 mm plate get 3 and 8 of them, 8 being the cap; a 200 x 200 mm plate gets `AnchorLarge` on every point; a point of the generator on a flat area becomes the anchor instead of a new one beside it; no anchor stands on a surface that faces away from the plate (a plate with a wall standing on it); the same model gets the same anchors on a second run; and the whole path of the support tool on the miniature. `SlaSupportRolesTests.cpp` gains the new role and its T0.6 class, and the 3MF round trip of a support point gains a third point carrying it, since every role is written through the name table of the writer. NOT CHECKED in a running app. Two things this pass leaves as they are, both on purpose: the anchors are placed by the support tool and not by the support step of a slice (`slaposSupportPoints` is untouched, as the roles of M7.8.2 are), and the auto support classifier of M2.37 still sizes a point by the base and detail bands it has, so the class of a role is asked of `rulebook_tip_class` where the tool asks for it and is not written onto the point by the engine.
  - [x] **M7.8.4** Raft against suction (R6): a raft type Auto, the default, that builds no raft unless the part's underside would form a suction cup against the plate, and then builds one. · M · needs M7.5
    Result: raft_type grew an Auto, which is the default of a new config and the first row of the combobox ("Auto (raft only against suction)"); what a preset or a project stores is the NAME of the type, never its number, so the four shapes from before it are untouched and the shipped SLA print presets (which still name pad_enable) keep the full plate raft they always printed. The rule itself is one pure function, sla::auto_raft_decision() in the new libslic3r/SLA/RaftAuto.{hpp,cpp}: the mesh in the print pose, the layer height and the elevation the part prints at without a raft, run through the same post-slice cup detection the peel force estimate uses (SLA/CavityDetection.hpp), so the rule and the peel force cannot disagree about what a suction cup is. A part that stands on the plate gets the raft AROUND the object (a full plate one has no tree to hang on, which generate_pad() gives up on and sla_pad_robustness_tests pins) and a part held above the plate gets the FULL plate one, the slab its pillars stand on, whose top skin is what the pocket opens onto. The decision is taken once per object, at the top of the object slice step in SLAPrint (the raft decides the elevation, so nothing may read the elevation before it), and the support tool takes the same function for the preview, so the preview and the print cannot show two different rafts: libslic3r/SLASupportTool.hpp reports the resolved raft and the elevation it was built for, and the preview service places the nodes by that lift instead of by the one the config alone gives. An Auto nothing has resolved builds no raft, which is the safe half of the rule (R6.1). The Preview "Supports" section says in one line how many of the models on the plate the rule gave a raft, taken from the preview service through the navigator (no geometry is read on the main thread), and raft_type's visible rows for Auto are the union of the two outcomes it can resolve to. Tests: the new tests/sla_print/sla_raft_auto_tests.cpp (a solid block gets no raft, a cup standing open side down and a hollow sole both get one, None and Around object under the same cup are untouched, the slice and the preview resolve the same, an unresolved Auto builds no raft, a part held above the plate gets the slab), three cases in SlaRaftSettingsTests.cpp for the default, the combo order and the rows Auto shows, and sla_auto_raft_note() in SlaSupportsPanelTests.cpp. sla_robustness_tests.cpp, sla_layer_hash_tests.cpp and the benchmark comment now name raft_type where they mean a raft, since the default is no longer Full. NOT BUILT (a human builds and runs the tests). Known: the shipped community and prusa-research SLA print presets still carry pad_enable and are migrated to a full plate raft, so a real print gets Auto only from a preset that names it; and the one line covers the models the support preview has built, which are the ones with support points. Reconciled with sla/batch-m15 (the merge is committed here): generate_support_points_for_tool resolves the raft once at the top, scales the support curve with the size of the part (M7.8.7), moves the points onto the surface, classifies their roles (M7.8.2), puts the heavy anchors of M7.8.3 on the underside, and only then runs the zero-elevation filter - on the resolved raft and not on raft_type alone, so an Auto that found a suction cup under a part standing on the plate takes the anchors on the bottom of that part away, and an Auto that found no cup, or a part held above the plate, keeps them. The stops of M4.16 stay where that todo put them (both walks over the points ask every 4096 of them), and nothing else of this todo moved: the tool still reports the same resolved raft and the same elevation, and the raft helpers still take an unresolved Auto as the no raft of R6.1.

Nine errors, all of them in libslic3r/SLA/RaftAuto.{hpp,cpp}, no link error. · S · needs M7.8.4
    Result: by OpenCode, NOT BUILT (a human builds and runs the tests). No behaviour change: the layers the rule reads, the detection it runs them through and the raft it picks are exactly as M7.8.4 left them, only the names are real ones now. Five fixes. (1) `Domain::indexed_triangle_set` is not a type in this fork: admesh/stl.h declares `indexed_triangle_set` in the GLOBAL namespace and Slic3r::Domain only carries `indexed_triangle_set_with_color` (Slic3r/Domain/TriangleMesh.hpp:14), so the mesh parameter of `auto_raft_decision()` is the unqualified name, the way libslic3r/SLA/Pad.hpp:28, libslic3r/TriangleMeshSlicer.hpp:84 and SLAPrint.hpp:511 already write it and the only caller already writes it too (resolve_object_raft(), SLAPrint.cpp:163) - the declaration had to be the one the call site uses. (2) The two `using` declarations in the anonymous namespace of RaftAuto.cpp are gone with it. `using Domain::indexed_triangle_set;` WAS the C2039, and the `using Domain::ExPolygons;` beside it could no longer be trusted after it, which is the C2440: the layer vector now spells out the one type both ends of the hand-off use, `std::vector<Domain::ExPolygons>`, which is what `slice_mesh_ex()` returns (TriangleMeshSlicer.hpp:106) and what `detect_cavities()` takes (CavityDetection.hpp:70) - so nothing is converted and the C2440 goes with it. (`Slic3r::ExPolygons`, libslic3r/ExPolygon.hpp:27, is an alias of the same type and would have been right too; neither is the other.) (3) The cup detection lives in `Slic3r::SLA`, capital, not in `Slic3r::sla` where the resolver lives, so `CavityAnalysis`, `CavityDetectionOptions` and `CupHit` are `SLA::`-qualified now and the call is `SLA::detect_cavities()`; that is the same API and the same qualification the NoCups orient goal uses (OrientCrossSection.cpp:91), so the rule and the goal still read one cup definition. (4) tests/sla_print/sla_raft_auto_tests.cpp spelled that mesh type wrong in eleven places; the target never reached it, since libslic3r fails to build first, so it is fixed with the header. The file also takes Slic3r/Domain/TriangleMesh.hpp (indexed_triangle_set, its_volume) and Slic3r/Domain/Types.hpp (Vec3f, Transform3d) for the names it uses, since the brief is to include every header used. (5) One AGENTS.md trap in the same test: `CHECK(outcome.pad_enabled == (a && b))` is a named `expect_pad` beside the CHECK now, so no `&&` sits inside a CHECK. Every other API the test and the resolver use was read in the source before use and needed no change (MeshBoolean::cgal::minus takes an its, SLAPrintObject::object_raft()/pad_mesh()/config() and the four raft helpers are in SLAPrint.hpp, SlaSupportPreviewService and the App headers are untouched by this fix).
    Result: every container of the pass that is keyed by a cell of the spot grid goes through one alias now, `CellMap = std::unordered_map<Cell2, std::vector<int>, Cell2Hash>`, so none of them can be written without a hasher again. `Cell2Hash`, next to the `Cell2` it hashes, mixes the two cell indices into one bucket index the way Boost's `hash_combine` does (`x * 73856093 ^ y * 19349663`); both indices are small non-negative numbers, since the cells are counted from the corner of the model (`cell_index()`). The six sites are the map of facets by cell in `make_spots()`, the return type of `index_spots()` and its own map, the `const` reference parameter of `measure_flat_areas()`, the one of `demote_around()`, the map of anchors inside `AnchorIndex` and the one in `add_heavy_anchors()`. No anchor moves: which spot is chosen comes from `better_spot()`, which ends on the position of the spot itself and not on the order a map is walked in, and the cells hold nothing else. The whole file, the header and `tests/sla_print/sla_support_anchor_tests.cpp` were read for the rest of what a build stops on and nothing else was found: `Cell2` is the only pair in an unordered container here (the three unordered containers elsewhere in `libslic3r/SLA` key by `std::string`, `uint64_t` and `size_t`, which all have a standard hash), and the test keeps to the traps of AGENTS.md - no `||`, `&&` or ternary inside `CHECK`/`REQUIRE`, `catch_approx.hpp` for `Approx`, the `[] { return false; }` it hands `generate_support_points_for_tool` is a call argument and not a default argument, no lambda as a default member initializer anywhere, and Eigen never mixes `float` with `double`. One line of the same kind was spelled out: `add_heavy_anchors()` names the origin of the grid `Vec2d{lowest_x, lowest_y}` now, instead of leaving the Eigen type out of the braces of the aggregate init of `SpotGrid`, which then does not depend on Eigen's two-scalar constructor being non-explicit. NOT BUILT (a human builds and runs the tests).

  - [x] **M7.8.5** Detail gets the lightest tips (R4.9): points in detailed regions (fine, dense or highly curved surface) get T0.1 whatever their role, except the lowest-point anchor; the most common auto-support failure the maintainer sees is heavy supports in detail. · M · needs M7.8.2
    Result: by OpenCode, NOT BUILT (a human builds and runs the tests). `SLA::SupportPoint::Role` gains `Detail` at the end of the enum and never in the middle of it, so every table indexed by a role keeps the values it had; it is compared in `operator==` like the others and written out with them as `role`, under the name `detail` in the 3MF and in the undo snapshots. What a detailed region is gets decided in `sla::classify_support_point_roles()` (`SLA/SupportRoles.{hpp,cpp}`) as the last of the rules, on the surface and not on the mesh: the ray probes of R4.5 are walked out from the point to `detail_radius_mm` = 1.5 mm in 8 directions and 10 steps, and two numbers of the heights of the hits (how far each is from the plane through the point, perpendicular to its own normal) answer the two halves of R4.9. `sag` is the mean of those heights on the ring at the radius and is the "highly curved" half: it is nothing on a plate or a slope whatever the mesh is made of, r2 / 2R on a curve, and `detail_sag_mm` = 0.2 mm (a curvature radius of about 5.6 mm) is the line, so a 20 mm barrel is light and a 3 mm bead is detail. `turns` is how many highs and lows of its own the surface goes over between the point and that radius, averaged over the directions of the walk, and is the "fine or dense" half: a plain surface and a smooth curve only fall away from the plane and go over none, relief goes over one per feature whichever side of the plane it is on, and `detail_turns` = 2 is the line. A region the rays could not see into - the rim of a silhouette, the open side of a hollow, a spot over a gap, fewer than three spots of the ring - is not detailed either, since that is no evidence at all. Fragile is decided before this and stays Fragile (R4.4, the same minimum tip), and the Anchor of the lowest island is the one role R4.9 never replaces (R4.1), so an island, a small island or an overhang in detail becomes Detail and nothing else does; a point that R4.5 moved is asked again at the spot it holds. The cost is 80 ray cast probes per point that gets that far, where the R4.5 probes were already spending 24. App: `rulebook_tip_class(Detail)` answers "mini" (T0.1), and the generated-point fill of M2.37 gets a third bundle, `SlaAutoSupportPresets::minimum`, which `fill_generated_point_geometry()` fills from preset button 1 of the tool ("mini", T0.1 mm), so a generated point whose role is Detail takes that whole class - the ball contact sunk half of the 0.1 mm tip (R3.1), the cone, the hexagonal stem and the prism foot (R3) - through the one `apply_sla_support_preset()` a preset button uses as well, and a point of the base keeps the heavy class of R4.1 whatever its role says. `sla_auto_support_is_detailed()` is the predicate of that choice and reads the role alone, since the engine has already measured the surface under the point; no config key is added and no setting changes. Tests: three cases in `tests/sla_print/sla_support_roles_tests.cpp` on shapes written from scratch - the flat underside of a 6 x 6 x 2 mm plate is no detailed region and its points stay Anchor and Overhang, a plate of the same size with a field of 0.5 mm square pyramids 0.5 mm deep under it is one (the point of the lowest island stays Anchor, an island 4 mm above it and a slope point on the same relief both become Detail, and no point is Fragile and none is moved), and a smooth curve is detail only where it curves hard (a sphere of 20 mm radius stays Overhang, one of 3 mm becomes Detail); three cases in App/Plater, where `Detail` joins the roles the three enumerations of `SlaSupportRolesTests.cpp` name (which also fix the `tip_diameter_mm` of M7.8.2, a member `SlaSupportPreset` has never had, so that file has not compiled since), two new cases in `SlaSupportAutoPresetsTests.cpp` (a generated point in detail takes the T0.1 class with its 0.05 mm contact and its ball, the other generated points are untouched, and a point of the base keeps the heavy class), and the round trip of the name `detail` in `3MFTests.cpp`. NOT CHECKED in a running app and nothing sliced. Merged with `sla/batch-m15`, which had appended `AnchorLarge` (M7.8.3) at the same end of the enum while this branch was open. The enum is now Unknown, Anchor, Island, SmallIsland, Overhang, Fragile, AnchorLarge, Detail: `AnchorLarge` keeps the value it already had, so a project saved with it reads back as an anchor of a very large object and not as a detailed region, and `Detail` is the one appended after it. `rulebook_tip_class` gained the eighth entry (`AnchorLarge` -> "xheavy" kept, `Detail` -> "mini" added), `ROLE_NAMES` of the 3MF writer the same, and the tests of both sides kept: `SlaSupportRolesTests.cpp` names all eight roles and `3MFTests.cpp` round trips four points, one per role added after M7.8.2. The R4.9 guard in `classify_support_point_roles` now names both anchor roles through `is_anchor()` rather than only `Role::Anchor`, so an anchor of a very large object keeps its xheavy tip even if the two passes are ever run in the other order; the order itself (roles first, then `add_heavy_anchors`) is unchanged and is what keeps an anchor an anchor.
- [x] **M7.8.5-FixRoles4** Batch m15 follow-up: R4.4 thickness measure uses the part, not a sub-detail bump. When `probe_surface_around()` gives a local plane, also cast `thickness_at` along the inverted normal of that best-fit plane and use the larger of the two thicknesses for `is_fragile`. The relief plate fixture (2 mm plate with 0.5 mm pyramids) was fragile at pyramid apexes because the point normal is nearly horizontal there; the fitted plane normal is vertical and measures the 2.5 mm part thickness. Spike tips stay fragile (whole feature is thin, plane ray is thin too), stud/brim cases of R4.5 unchanged (moved points use single ray at new position), thin features of anchor tests unchanged (stud tips still thin). Product fix, test was correct. · S · needs M7.8.5
    Result: `thickness_plane` computed from fitted plane normal in `classify_support_point_roles()` (`SupportRoles.cpp:657-671`); `is_fragile` called with `thickness_plane` (line 696); moved points reset `thickness_plane` to single-ray thickness. All role tests pass.

  - [x] **M7.8.7** Density that scales with the size of the part: the largest radius one support point may hold is the smaller of the maximum of the support radius curve and a fixed fraction of the size of the object, calibrated on the studio supported miniature heads of `doc/sla-fork/supports/calibration.md` (R7.1, which keeps the spacing free of resin, printer and layer height). · M · needs M7.5, M7.8.1
    Result: by OpenCode, NOT BUILT (a human builds and runs the suite) and NOT CHECKED in a running app; the benchmark heads bm01/bm13 and the calibration heads are to be measured locally. The radius one point may hold was a constant of the program: the default support curve grows to 6 mm of surface on a point, which is right for a plate and which swallows a 9 mm miniature head whole in one or two points, where the studio that supports these models by hand in another slicer places 17 to 24 contact tips on a head of that size (M7.4c, five heads of 8.4 to 9.7 mm, median 20). It is a size of the part now, and nothing else. `sla::support_size_reference()` measures the object off the prepared layers as the smaller of its two bounding box extents in XY and the side of the square of its largest layer - 7.09 mm for the head, 40 mm for a 40 mm cube, 100 mm for a 100 mm plate - and `sla::support_curve_for_size()` scales every radius of `SupportPointGeneratorConfig::support_curve` by the one factor `support_curve_size_factor` = 0.36 times that size, or hands the curve back unchanged when the factor does not reach its 6 mm, which is what a big part is. Only x is scaled: the y of a curve point is the height over the support point its radius grows with, and a height is not a radius. Both places that assemble the config from prepared data scale it, `SLASupportTool.cpp` (the preview and the auto support of the tool) and `SLAPrintSteps.cpp` (the slice step), so the preview and the slice agree; `support_points_density_relative` still scales the result on top of it and is untouched. R7.1 holds: the factor reads the shape of the object and nothing else - no resin, no printer, no layer height - and support_curve_size_factor is a calibration of the M7.4c numbers with the note in `SupportPointGenerator.hpp` pointing at the table it was read from. Tests in `tests/sla_print/sla_support_size_density_tests.cpp`: a synthetic head written from scratch (a sphere of 8 mm on a 1 mm neck cylinder, 8.6 mm tall, sliced at 0.05 mm) takes 15 to 25 points with the default config, the middle of the window the studio data gives, and the same shape gives the same points twice; a 40 mm cube, a 100 mm plate and a cone 40 mm across (a cone because a box standing on the plate has no overhang at all and so cannot tell whether the curve of a big part was left alone) keep the points they had - exactly, which is inside the 10 % the todo asks for - measured by running the generator over their own layers once with the curve of before and once with the curve the size asks for; the curve itself is checked to scale every radius by one factor, to leave the heights alone and to be the curve of before for a part that is big enough; and the size of the head, of the cube and of the plate is pinned. No existing generator test pinned an exact count on a small model, so none of them had to change. What this does move is every small model: the benchmark heads bm01 and bm13 of a similar size get a denser support than before, and whether that is enough is what the local run of the calibration heads decides - M7.11 tunes the rest of the density against the scorecard (M7.7).
- [x] **M7.8.8** The roles decide the tip classes of the generated points: a point of a known role takes the whole class of its role (anchor heavy, anchor of a very large object xheavy, island medium, small island and overhang light, fragile mini), with the two settings of M2.37 on top. · M · needs M7.8.2, M7.8.3
    Result: by OpenCode, NOT BUILT (a human builds and runs the tests). The roles of M7.8.2 now reach the sizes, which was the gap M7.8.3's result named: the one place that sizes a generation, `sla_apply_auto_support_presets` in `SlaSupportAutoPresets.cpp` (which both generation paths of `SlaSupportPointsGizmo::fill_generated_point_geometry` go through), asks the new pure rule `sla_role_tip_class(role, heavy_base, detail)` of `SlaSupportRoles.{hpp,cpp}` - next to the `rulebook_tip_class` of M7.8.2, which it is the sized form of - and writes the class it names with `apply_sla_support_preset`, so a ball contact sunk half its own tip (R3.1), the cone under it (R3.2), the hexagonal stem (R3.3) and the 6 x 0.3 mm prism foot (R3.4) land on a generated point exactly as a preset button lands them, and the four sizes of the print preset come with them. The rule: Fragile is mini whatever the settings ask for, since a thick support under a thin feature snaps it off (R4.4, R4.5); Anchor and AnchorLarge take their own class while `support_auto_heavy_base` is on and the detail preset when it is off, since with the heavy base off nothing tells the base of the model apart; Island keeps the medium tip of R4.3 unless the detail preset is lighter, which is the whole range `support_auto_detail_preset` offers; Overhang and SmallIsland take the detail preset, whatever it names (R4.3, R4.6). A point of `Role::Unknown` - a project written before the roles existed, a path that does not classify - keeps M2.37's band rule exactly, and a point of the user is never touched whatever role it carries. The values of a class still come from the print preset of the printer, not only from the config definitions: `SlaAutoSupportPresets` gained the five classes of the tool (`classes`, indexed by `sla_support_preset_name`, with `class_of()` resolving a name and an unknown name falling back to the largest class as `sla_support_preset()` does) and the gizmo fills all five from `get_support_preset_values` rather than the two of M2.37, so the per-printer diameters of a vendor bundle still reach every class a role may pick; `base` and `detail` stay, and are two of the five. Nothing else of a point is touched: where it stands, what it is and how it rests on the model are not values of a class. Tests: `SlaSupportRolesTests.cpp` covers the rule exhaustively, every role x heavy base on and off x every detail choice, against a table written from the rulebook rather than from the rule (an unclassified point gets no class at all, and a role of a build that knew more of them still gets one); `SlaSupportAutoPresetsTests.cpp` covers the write - a role beats the height of the band, the two settings land on top of it, a support of the user is untouched - and the lookup of a class by its name; the new `SlaSupportRoleFillTests.cpp` is the flow, a model written from scratch (a slab on the plate, a mushroom whose cap floats 8 mm above it, a spike too thin to carry anything but the minimum tip) generated with `generate_support_points_for_tool` and filled the way the gizmo fills it, with every point's tip, stem, foot and contact depth checked against its role, for the three detail presets and with the heavy base off, and with the band rule pinned for a point that has no role.
